```text
docs/paper/sections/abstract_body.tex:1:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex:4:We identify trained $\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of CIVO in DreamerV3 (26/29, AUROC${=}0.953$; LOEO cross-validated). No tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO; in pure imagination, dual critics converge ($\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective. The feedback architecture---MPC re-planning versus imagination-based bootstrapping---is the structural factor.
docs/paper/sections/methodology.tex:38:We define $\nve_t = (v/e)_{\text{config},t} / (v/e)_{\text{baseline},t}$, where $v/e$ is the critic's mean imagined value divided by mean evaluation return, and the baseline is the highest-performing seed (exp\_001, seed~1, 505K steps). We restrict to a \textbf{35K+ window} to avoid early-training inflation. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold achieves 26/29 accuracy (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, mean AUROC${=}0.981$, Appendix~Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).

docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:231:The original $n{=}16$ analysis (Tables~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}29$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:236:\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}29$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}

270:\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).
277:To assess whether $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}13$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\theta{=}1.5\times$ on the held-out environment.

docs/paper/sections/analysis.tex:8:Monitor via NVE diagnostic ($\geq 1.5\times$, AUROC${=}0.953$; Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}). Freezing the $\dt$-embedding converts CIVO onset (7/7 trained locomotion, NVE $1.53$--$108\times$) to transient spikes that self-correct within 20K steps (2/3 frozen Walker, $1.71$--$1.88\times$; 0/2 frozen Cheetah); untreated, extreme CIVO ($70.6\times$) causes irrecoverable damage (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_analysis}).
docs/paper/sections/abstract_body.tex:1:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex:4:We identify trained $\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of CIVO in DreamerV3 (26/29, AUROC${=}0.953$; LOEO cross-validated). No tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO; in pure imagination, dual critics converge ($\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective. The feedback architecture---MPC re-planning versus imagination-based bootstrapping---is the structural factor.
docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex:10:Statistical power with 4 seeds per configuration is limited \citep{colas2018seeds}; the NVE threshold (LOEO AUROC${=}0.981$) may require recalibration for different reward scales.
docs/paper/sections/methodology.tex:38:We define $\nve_t = (v/e)_{\text{config},t} / (v/e)_{\text{baseline},t}$, where $v/e$ is the critic's mean imagined value divided by mean evaluation return, and the baseline is the highest-performing seed (exp\_001, seed~1, 505K steps). We restrict to a \textbf{35K+ window} to avoid early-training inflation. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold achieves 26/29 accuracy (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, mean AUROC${=}0.981$, Appendix~Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:150:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:154:\caption{\textbf{Task~B1}: Sustained CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\text{pos}}=3$, $n_{\text{neg}}=7$, $n=10$, $\text{AUROC}=1.000$). All confidence intervals are two-sided Clopper-Pearson exact binomial ($\alpha=0.05$). $^\dagger$Optimal threshold in $(3.78, 4.02]$.}
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:176:\caption{\textbf{Task~B2}: Any CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\text{pos}}=4$, $n_{\text{neg}}=6$, $n=10$, $\text{AUROC}=0.958$). The single false positive at $\geq 1.5\times$ is 008e\_v3\_s2 (IRD mechanism, not CIVO). The $1.5\times$ threshold (highlighted) maximizes F1 while maintaining perfect recall.}
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:197:\caption{\textbf{Task~B2 (full evaluation set)}: Any CIVO detection across two environments ($n_{\text{pos}}=8$, $n_{\text{neg}}=8$, $n=16$, $\text{AUROC}=0.969$). Includes Walker Walk (14 configurations) and Cheetah Run (2 configurations).}
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:236:\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}29$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}

Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:272:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:277:To assess whether $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}13$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\theta{=}1.5\times$ on the held-out environment.

The feedback architecture---MPC re-planning versus imagination-based bootstrapping---is the structural factor.
docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex:10:Statistical power with 4 seeds per configuration is limited \citep{colas2018seeds}; the NVE threshold (LOEO AUROC${=}0.981$) may require recalibration for different reward scales.

docs/paper/sections/results.tex:72:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/sections/methodology.tex:38:We define $\nve_t = (v/e)_{\text{config},t} / (v/e)_{\text{baseline},t}$, where $v/e$ is the critic's mean imagined value divided by mean evaluation return, and the baseline is the highest-performing seed (exp\_001, seed~1, 505K steps). We restrict to a \textbf{35K+ window} to avoid early-training inflation. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold achieves 26/29 accuracy (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, mean AUROC${=}0.981$, Appendix~Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).

```text
1	World-model reinforcement learning is increasingly deployed for robot control, where temporal abstraction enables planning across heterogeneous time scales. We find that $\Delta t$-conditioning, effective in model-predictive control (MPC), cannot transfer to imagination-based actor-critic training: every variable-rate DreamerV3 configuration degrades to 14--19\% of baseline. Factorial diagnostics on two continuous-control tasks isolate trained temporal embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of this failure---\emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO; 26/29, AUROC${=}0.953$, LOEO cross-validated). The RSSM develops a $2.78\times$ reward prediction gap at $\Delta t{>}1$ that the critic internalizes as optimistic values; the contrast with MPC identifies feedback architecture as the structural factor. No tested intervention across four levels (gradient, output, representation, architecture) reliably eliminates CIVO: dual critics converge in pure imagination ($\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective, though min $+$ increased capacity enables recovery. A lightweight NVE detector achieves 100\% sensitivity.
2	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	World-model reinforcement learning enables sample-efficient learning directly on physical hardware \citep{wu2023daydreamer, hansen2024tdmpc2}. Real robotic tasks demand temporal abstraction: manipulation requires fast reactive grasping alongside slow approach planning, locomotion involves gait phases at different frequencies, and sim-to-real transfer must handle $\dt$ mismatches between simulation and hardware. TAWM \citep{nhu2025tawm} shows that conditioning a world model on step size $\dt$ yields consistent gains within TD-MPC2's MPC framework \citep{hansen2024tdmpc2}. Can the same $\dt$-conditioning bring temporal abstraction to imagination-based actor-critic methods like DreamerV3 \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, where policies are trained entirely in imagined rollouts?
5	
6	We find that it cannot. Every variable-rate configuration degrades performance catastrophically (14--19\% of baseline). Through a factorial analysis on Walker Walk \citep{tassa2018dmcontrol}, we isolate the combination of a \emph{trained} $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor. This combination enables the RSSM reward head to develop a $2.78\times$ prediction gap between $\dt{=}1$ and $\dt{=}2$, which the critic internalizes as optimistic value estimates, a failure mode we term \emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO), analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional} but model-induced rather than policy-class-induced. A normalized value-to-eval (NVE) threshold of $\geq 1.5\times$ achieves 26/29 classification ($n{=}29$, two environments, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, Appendix~Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).
7	Gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude does not predict causal importance: the reward head contributes 38--49\% of $\nabla_\theta e_{\dt}$ yet reward-only stopping fails, while the observation decoder (16--22\%) carries the critical signal. A four-level intervention hierarchy (gradient, output, representation, architecture) shows that no tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO across environments. In pure-imagination training, dual critics converge to identical value functions ($\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective; the min operation nonetheless enables CIVO recovery, while prevention requires min $+$ increased capacity. These findings are directly relevant to robotics: as world-model RL moves toward real systems with variable-rate sensing and multi-timescale control, understanding when temporal conditioning fails, and why standard countermeasures cannot fix it, is essential for safe deployment.
8	
9	\paragraph{Contributions.}
10	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
11	    \item \textbf{Failure mode identification.} We identify CIVO as specific to imagination-based actor-critic with temporal conditioning and isolate trained $\dt$-embedding $+$ multi-rate exposure as jointly sufficient in tested configurations (7/7 seeds, two locomotion environments, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated). Cross-environment validation spanning locomotion and manipulation (ManiSkill) establishes that CIVO is locomotion-specific; frozen-embedding controls on three manipulation tasks identify value-ratio transients as environment-driven, demonstrating the diagnostic's specificity on non-CIVO dynamics.
12	    \item \textbf{Architectural boundary.} $\dt$-conditioning succeeds in TD-MPC2's MPC re-planning but fails under imagination-based bootstrapping, where model errors compound into biased value targets with no real-data anchor.
13	    \item \textbf{Four-level intervention hierarchy.} Across gradient, output, representation, and architecture levels, no intervention reliably eliminates CIVO across environments. Gradient magnitude does not predict causal importance (reward head: 38--49\%, reward-only stopping fails). In pure-imagination training, dual critics converge ($\rho > 0.999$), eliminating Double Q pessimism, though the min operation enables recovery through a different mechanism.
14	    \item \textbf{Online detection.} A lightweight NVE-based detector achieves 100\% sensitivity (5/5) with predictive warning in 2/5 cases.
15	\end{enumerate}
16	
```

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:methodology}
3	
4	\subsection{Base Architecture}
5	\label{sec:base_arch}
6	
7	All experiments build on DreamerV3 \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, which learns a recurrent state-space model (RSSM) with discrete latent states ($32$ categories $\times$ $32$ classes), uses symlog-transformed predictions, and trains an actor-critic entirely within imagined trajectories. The standard configuration imagines $H{=}15$ steps at a fixed temporal resolution of $\dt{=}1$, meaning each imagination step corresponds to one environment transition.
8	
9	\subsection{Variable-Rate Imagination Mechanism}
10	\label{sec:variable_rate}
11	
12	We introduce three orthogonal architectural modifications that enable variable-rate imagination in DreamerV3. Each can be independently toggled to isolate its contribution.
13	
14	\paragraph{Temporal embedding.}
15	A learned embedding $e(\dt)$ is concatenated to the RSSM input at each imagination step to condition state predictions on the temporal scale. We test three modes:
16	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
17	\item \textbf{Trained}: embedding parameters are updated via backpropagation through the world model loss, so the RSSM can co-adapt its dynamics and reward predictions to the temporal scale.
18	\item \textbf{Frozen}: a fixed random projection of identical dimensionality (\texttt{requires\_grad=False}). The RSSM receives a temporal signal but cannot co-adapt to it.
19	\item \textbf{Removed}: embedding dimension set to zero; no temporal conditioning signal is provided.
20	\end{itemize}
21	We further distinguish \textbf{trained-and-exposed} (subsample ON: the RSSM trains on $\dt{>}1$ transitions) from \textbf{trained-but-unexposed} (subsample OFF: $\dt{>}1$ used only during imagination). This four-way classification (trained/exposed, trained/unexposed, frozen, removed) is the key experimental structure of our factorial design.
22	
23	\paragraph{Temporal subsampling.}
24	When enabled, the replay buffer augments training data with variable-$\dt$ transitions by subsampling consecutive frames. Concretely, with a maximum subsampling factor of~2, the RSSM trains on both $\dt{=}1$ and $\dt{=}2$ transitions drawn from the same episodes. When disabled, only standard $\dt{=}1$ transitions are used. This factor controls whether the world model has seen multi-scale dynamics during training.
25	
26	\paragraph{Imagination horizon and step size.}
27	The imagination horizon $H$ sets the number of rollout steps. We test $H{=}15$ (default) and $H{=}8$. The reduced horizon controls for compounding error: $H{=}8$ steps at $\dt{=}2$ covers $16$ physical time steps, comparable to the $15$ physical steps of the default configuration. The parameter \texttt{gate\_fixed\_dt} forces all imagination steps to use a specific $\dt$ value ($1$, $2$, or $4$); this separates the effect of $\dt$-conditioning from any learned gating policy.
28	
29	\subsection{Factorial Design}
30	\label{sec:factorial}
31	
32	We adopt a factorial ablation across four factors: $\dt$-embedding mode (trained / frozen / none), temporal subsampling (on / off), imagination horizon ($H{=}15$ / $H{=}8$), and imagination step size ($\dt{=}1$, $2$, $4$). The design (12 conditions, Table~\ref{tab:factorial_design} in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}) isolates trained $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as orthogonal factors. Key configurations use 4 seeds; following \citet{agarwal2021precipice}, we report per-seed trajectories.
33	
34	\subsection{Diagnostic Metrics}
35	\label{sec:metrics}
36	
37	\paragraph{Normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\nve$).}
38	We define $\nve_t = (v/e)_{\text{config},t} / (v/e)_{\text{baseline},t}$, where $v/e$ is the critic's mean imagined value divided by mean evaluation return, and the baseline is the highest-performing seed (exp\_001, seed~1, 505K steps). We restrict to a \textbf{35K+ window} to avoid early-training inflation. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold achieves 26/29 accuracy (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, mean AUROC${=}0.981$, Appendix~Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).
39	\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping}
40	\label{sec:sgs_method}
41	
42	To identify which gradient pathway through $e_{\Delta t}$ drives CIVO, we introduce \textit{selective gradient stopping} (SGS): $e_{\Delta t}$ is detached via $\operatorname{sg}(\cdot)$ before selected prediction heads during the backward pass, preventing those loss gradients from updating $e_{\Delta t}$ while preserving its forward-pass contribution. We test three variants: reward-only (exp\_016e), full-pathway (exp\_018, all non-KL gradients blocked), and obs-decoder-only (exp\_025/026). Gradient decomposition using $\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}_i$ quantifies each pathway's contribution (Appendix~\ref{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}).
43	
44	\subsection{Forward-Pass Interventions}
45	\label{sec:forward_pass_method}
46	
47	We test two forward-pass interventions as representation- and output-level probes: \textbf{TCN} adds Gaussian noise $\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$ to $e_{\Delta t}$ during imagination ($\sigma \in \{0.5, 0.2\}$, three environments); \textbf{RAVE} clips imagined values exceeding an EMA anchor ($c \in \{2.0, 1.5\}$).
48	
49	\subsection{Dual-Critic Mechanism Ablation}
50	\label{sec:dual_critic_method}
51	
52	To test whether standard overestimation countermeasures address CIVO, we conduct a $2{\times}2$ factorial: \{single, dual\} $\times$ \{narrow (${\sim}1.2$M), wide (${\sim}2.4$M)\} critic parameters. The dual-critic variant follows TD3 \citep{fujimoto2018td3}: two independent value heads trained on shared RSSM features, with the actor optimizing $\min(v_1, v_2)$. The wide variant doubles hidden dimensions to control for capacity independently of the min operation.
53	
54	\subsection{Environment and Training Details}
55	\label{sec:env_details}
56	
57	All experiments use Walker Walk and Cheetah Run from the DeepMind Control Suite (DMC; \citealt{tassa2018dmcontrol}) with proprioceptive observations, chosen for their contrasting dynamics (6-joint biped vs.\ 2-joint runner). Walker Walk serves as the primary environment with the full factorial design; Cheetah Run serves as a severity-dependent generalization probe: CIVO manifests at $8$--$70\times$ NVE on Cheetah versus $2$--$5\times$ on Walker, testing whether interventions transfer under amplified severity rather than replicating the full factorial. The baseline runs for 500K steps (peak return 970.4); treatments run for 105K steps, sufficient to observe collapse onset. All hyperparameters follow the DreamerV3 defaults \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3} without task-specific tuning ($d{=}32$ temporal embedding; full hyperparameters in Appendix~\ref{sec:hyperparameters}). Training uses a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (${\sim}80$ GPU-hours total). The codebase extends the official DreamerV3 implementation\footnote{\url{https://github.com/danijar/dreamerv3}, accessed March 2026.} with the variable-rate modules described in \S\ref{sec:variable_rate}.
58	
```

      "content": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:methodology}\n\n\\subsection{Base Architecture}\n\\label{sec:base_arch}\n\nAll experiments build on DreamerV3 \\citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, which learns a recurrent state-space model (RSSM) with discrete latent states ($32$ categories $\\times$ $32$ classes), uses symlog-transformed predictions, and trains an actor-critic entirely within imagined trajectories. The standard configuration imagines $H{=}15$ steps at a fixed temporal resolution of $\\dt{=}1$, meaning each imagination step corresponds to one environment transition.\n\n\\subsection{Variable-Rate Imagination Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:variable_rate}\n\nWe introduce three orthogonal architectural modifications that enable variable-rate imagination in DreamerV3. Each can be independently toggled to isolate its contribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal embedding.}\nA learned embedding $e(\\dt)$ is concatenated to the RSSM input at each imagination step to condition state predictions on the temporal scale. We test three modes:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Trained}: embedding parameters are updated via backpropagation through the world model loss, so the RSSM can co-adapt its dynamics and reward predictions to the temporal scale.\n\\item \\textbf{Frozen}: a fixed random projection of identical dimensionality (\\texttt{requires\\_grad=False}). The RSSM receives a temporal signal but cannot co-adapt to it.\n\\item \\textbf{Removed}: embedding dimension set to zero; no temporal conditioning signal is provided.\n\\end{itemize}\nWe further distinguish \\textbf{trained-and-exposed} (subsample ON: the RSSM trains on $\\dt{>}1$ transitions) from \\textbf{trained-but-unexposed} (subsample OFF: $\\dt{>}1$ used only during imagination). This four-way classification (trained/exposed, trained/unexposed, frozen, removed) is the key experimental structure of our factorial design.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal subsampling.}\nWhen enabled, the replay buffer augments training data with variable-$\\dt$ transitions by subsampling consecutive frames. Concretely, with a maximum subsampling factor of~2, the RSSM trains on both $\\dt{=}1$ and $\\dt{=}2$ transitions drawn from the same episodes. When disabled, only standard $\\dt{=}1$ transitions are used. This factor controls whether the world model has seen multi-scale dynamics during training.\n\n\\paragraph{Imagination horizon and step size.}\nThe imagination horizon $H$ sets the number of rollout steps. We test $H{=}15$ (default) and $H{=}8$. The reduced horizon controls for compounding error: $H{=}8$ steps at $\\dt{=}2$ covers $16$ physical time steps, comparable to the $15$ physical steps of the default configuration. The parameter \\texttt{gate\\_fixed\\_dt} forces all imagination steps to use a specific $\\dt$ value ($1$, $2$, or $4$); this separates the effect of $\\dt$-conditioning from any learned gating policy.\n\n\\subsection{Factorial Design}\n\\label{sec:factorial}\n\nWe adopt a factorial ablation across four factors: $\\dt$-embedding mode (trained / frozen / none), temporal subsampling (on / off), imagination horizon ($H{=}15$ / $H{=}8$), and imagination step size ($\\dt{=}1$, $2$, $4$). The design (12 conditions, Table~\\ref{tab:factorial_design} in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}) isolates trained $\\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as orthogonal factors. Key configurations use 4 seeds; following \\citet{agarwal2021precipice}, we report per-seed trajectories.\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\paragraph{Normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\\nve$).}\nWe define $\\nve_t = (v/e)_{\\text{config},t} / (v/e)_{\\text{baseline},t}$, where $v/e$ is the critic's mean imagined value divided by mean evaluation return, and the baseline is the highest-performing seed (exp\\_001, seed~1, 505K steps). We restrict to a \\textbf{35K+ window} to avoid early-training inflation. The $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold achieves 26/29 accuracy (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, mean AUROC${=}0.981$, Appendix~Table~\\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).\n\\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping}\n\\label{sec:sgs_method}\n\nTo identify which gradient pathway through $e_{\\Delta t}$ drives CIVO, we introduce \\textit{selective gradient stopping} (SGS): $e_{\\Delta t}$ is detached via $\\operatorname{sg}(\\cdot)$ before selected prediction heads during the backward pass, preventing those loss gradients from updating $e_{\\Delta t}$ while preserving its forward-pass contribution. We test three variants: reward-only (exp\\_016e), full-pathway (exp\\_018, all non-KL gradients blocked), and obs-decoder-only (exp\\_025/026). Gradient decomposition using $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{L}_i$ quantifies each pathway's contribution (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Forward-Pass Interventions}\n\\label{sec:forward_pass_method}\n\nWe test two forward-pass interventions as representation- and output-level probes: \\textbf{TCN} adds Gaussian noise $\\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$ to $e_{\\Delta t}$ during imagination ($\\sigma \\in \\{0.5, 0.2\\}$, three environments); \\textbf{RAVE} clips imagined values exceeding an EMA anchor ($c \\in \\{2.0, 1.5\\}$).\n\n\\subsection{Dual-Critic Mechanism Ablation}\n\\label{sec:dual_critic_method}\n\nTo test whether standard overestimation countermeasures address CIVO, we conduct a $2{\\times}2$ factorial: \\{single, dual\\} $\\times$ \\{narrow (${\\sim}1.2$M), wide (${\\sim}2.4$M)\\} critic parameters. The dual-critic variant follows TD3 \\citep{fujimoto2018td3}: two independent value heads trained on shared RSSM features, with the actor optimizing $\\min(v_1, v_2)$. The wide variant doubles hidden dimensions to control for capacity independently of the min operation.\n\n\\subsection{Environment and Training Details}\n\\label{sec:env_details}\n\nAll experiments use Walker Walk and Cheetah Run from the DeepMind Control Suite (DMC; \\citealt{tassa2018dmcontrol}) with proprioceptive observations, chosen for their contrasting dynamics (6-joint biped vs.\\ 2-joint runner). Walker Walk serves as the primary environment with the full factorial design; Cheetah Run serves as a severity-dependent generalization probe: CIVO manifests at $8$--$70\\times$ NVE on Cheetah versus $2$--$5\\times$ on Walker, testing whether interventions transfer under amplified severity rather than replicating the full factorial. The baseline runs for 500K steps (peak return 970.4); treatments run for 105K steps, sufficient to observe collapse onset. All hyperparameters follow the DreamerV3 defaults \\citep{hafner2023dreamerv3} without task-specific tuning ($d{=}32$ temporal embedding; full hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{sec:hyperparameters}). Training uses a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (${\\sim}80$ GPU-hours total). The codebase extends the official DreamerV3 implementation\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/danijar/dreamerv3}, accessed March 2026.} with the variable-rate modules described in \\S\\ref{sec:variable_rate}.\n",
      "numLines": 58,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 58
    }
  }
}
```

2	\label{sec:results}
3

We report evaluation returns (mean over 10 episodes) and the normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\nve$, \S\ref{sec:metrics}).

49	%% ============================================================
50	%% F1: All dt>1 degrade
51	%% ============================================================
52	
53	\subsection{F1: All Variable-Rate Configurations Degrade}
54	\label{sec:f1}
55	
56	Every configuration that introduces $\dt > 1$ imagination degrades relative to the $\dt{=}1$ baseline (Figure~\ref{fig:learning_curves}). The baseline reaches 915.7 at 105K; fixed $\dt{=}2$ (006) peaks at 485.3 before collapsing to 132.9 ($14\%$ of baseline); fixed $\dt{=}4$ (007) never exceeds 176.9 ($19\%$); the learned gate (005) collapses to 98.8 ($11\%$).
57	
58	\begin{figure}[t]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_learning_curves.pdf}
61	\caption{Learning curves. The baseline (black) reaches 915.7; all variable-rate configurations degrade.}
62	\label{fig:learning_curves}
63	\end{figure}
64	
65	%% ============================================================
66	%% F2: Trained dt_embedding → nve > 1.5x
67	%% ============================================================
68	
69	\subsection{F2: Trained Temporal Embedding Precedes Value Overestimation}
70	\label{sec:f2}
71	
72	The factorial design isolates trained $\dt$-embedding as the strongest predictor. The \emph{primary} comparison---008d (trained, subsample ON) vs.\ 008f (no embedding, subsample ON)---differs only in embedding training, yielding $\nve = 4.12\times$ (eval 82.8) vs.\ $\nve \leq 0.97\times$ (eval 503.0). \emph{Secondary} comparisons against 008a/008c (unexposed embedding) co-vary subsample status and horizon alongside the embedding factor. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 26 of 29 experimental configurations across two environments (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}; Figure~\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).
73	\begin{figure}[t]
74	\centering
75	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}
76	\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\pm$ seed range). Trained $\dt$-embedding (red, Walker Walk, $n{=}3$ seeds plotted; seed~4 data in Table~\ref{tab:complete_results}) exceeds the $1.5\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}
77	\label{fig:nve_trajectory}
78	\end{figure}
79	
80	The trained $\dt$-embedding precedes CIVO in all five Walker Walk seeds (peak NVE: $4.72\times$, $4.62\times$, $4.12\times$, $1.62\times$, $1.53\times$), universally degrading performance to below 30\% of baseline (IQM $175.2$, mean $170.6$, $n{=}5$; \citealt{agarwal2021precipice}). Downstream trajectories diverge: two seeds undergo full collapse, one exhibits late-onset collapse still declining at 105K, and two self-correct (seeds 2 and 5). Combined with Cheetah Run (\S\ref{sec:f6}), CIVO onset is universal across all seven tested seeds ($n{=}7$, two environments) with 5/7 progressing toward collapse, 2/7 self-correcting (Appendix~\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).
81	
82	%% ============================================================
83	%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)
84	%% ============================================================
85	
86	\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}
87	\label{sec:f3f5_summary}
88	
89	Trained $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure are \emph{jointly sufficient in tested configurations} for CIVO. Neither factor alone precedes pathological overestimation: trained embedding without multi-rate exposure (008a, 3 seeds, peak NVE $0.83$--$0.93\times$) and multi-rate exposure without embedding (008f, 3 seeds, peak NVE $\leq 1.41\times$) both remain below $1.5\times$. Cross-environment replication on Cheetah Run confirms generalization. Freezing the embedding (\texttt{requires\_grad=False}) dramatically reduces severity (NVE $0.19$--$1.88\times$ vs.\ trained $1.53$--$4.72\times$; 2/4 seeds below threshold, 2/4 transient self-correcting spikes; Table~\ref{tab:three_way_emb}); removing the embedding (008f) also avoids CIVO. Partial gradient blocking (exp\_044) does not prevent onset (peak NVE $4.45\times$) but permits delayed self-correction (final eval 538, 59\% baseline; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), confirming that \emph{any} gradient pathway updating $e_{\dt}$ suffices to induce CIVO. Degradation scales monotonically with $\dt$ ($-47\%$ at $\dt{=}2$, $-81\%$ at $\dt{=}4$; Figure~\ref{fig:dt_amplification}).
90	
91	\paragraph{ManiSkill manipulation tasks.}
92	None of three ManiSkill environments exhibit $\dt$-embedding-dependent CIVO. StackCube-v1 value-ratio spikes are environment-specific: trained (049/052, NVE $5.05$/$4.67\times$) and frozen (051, $4.30\times$) match, all self-correcting by 82.5K. PegInsertionSide-v1 exhibits high-amplitude stochastic dynamics ($4$--$11\times$) whose trained/frozen ordering is seed-dependent: seed~0 trained peaks higher ($10.05\times$ vs.\ frozen $1.82\times$), but seed~1 reverses (frozen peak $11.32\times$ vs.\ trained peak $3.08\times$). PickCube-v1 frozen control (exp\_054, peak $8.81\times$ at 62.5K, self-correcting) confirms the same pattern. CIVO is locomotion-specific in DreamerV3; the frozen-embedding diagnostic correctly identifies these transients as non-CIVO.
93	
94	%% ============================================================
95	%% F6--F7: Interventions and cross-environment
96	%% ============================================================
97	
98	\subsection{F6--F7: Interventions and Multiple Pathways}
99	\label{sec:f6}
100	
101	\paragraph{F6: Trained embedding creates multiple vulnerability pathways.}
102	Exp\_008e\_v3 restricts imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding. Two of four seeds collapse (50\%): Pathway~A (CIVO, $\nve = 4.02\times$) and preliminary Pathway~B (entropy \emph{increase}, $\nve = 3.78\times$; 2 seeds only, requires confirmation; Appendix~\ref{sec:pathways}). Pathway~B produces one of three non-CIVO false positives ($n{=}29$).
103	\paragraph{F7: Full-pathway gradient stopping shows mixed results.}
104	Full-SGS (exp\_018) detaches all non-KL gradients from $e_{\dt}$. Seed~1 shows partial mitigation (eval 448.3, NVE $1.66\times$); seed~2 collapses (0/2 elimination; Appendix~\ref{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah (010b, NVE $8.27$--$108.52\times$) confirms CIVO generalization; full-SGS yields 0/3 elimination. Removing the embedding eliminates CIVO on Cheetah (024b, NVE $< 0.2\times$).
105	
106	\paragraph{Targeted obs-decoder-only SGS confirms pathway redundancy.}
107	Obs-decoder-only SGS (exp\_025/026) achieves 1/6 CIVO prevention (1/4 Walker, 0/2 Cheetah; Table~\ref{tab:intervention_hierarchy}), not meaningfully different from full-pathway SGS (0/3). CIVO operates through redundant gradient channels: blocking any single pathway shifts co-adaptation to the remaining ones (Appendix~\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).
108

148	%% ============================================================
149	%% Forward-pass interventions
150	%% ============================================================
151	
152	\subsection{Forward-Pass Interventions}
153	\label{sec:forward_results}
154	
155	We test two forward-pass alternatives (Table~\ref{tab:intervention_hierarchy}). \textbf{RAVE} (value-level capping) fails: the EMA anchor tracks biased values during CIVO onset. \textbf{TCN} (representation-level noise) suppresses CIVO in 2 of 4 Walker seeds at $\sigma{=}0.2$ (NVE $< 1.0\times$, eval 45--91\%), but remaining seeds show runaway escalation, suggesting TCN gates rather than attenuates CIVO. Cross-environment transfer fails: Cheetah effective dose ($\sigma{=}1.0$) causes policy erosion to 15\% baseline; PickCube 0/1 (Appendix~\ref{sec:tcn_cross_env}). TCN is a \emph{diagnostic probe}, not a deployable mitigation.
156	
157	%% ============================================================
158	%% Architecture-Level Ablation
159	%% ============================================================
160	
161	\subsection{Architecture-Level Ablation}
162	\label{sec:arch_results}
163	
164	\paragraph{Critic convergence in pure imagination.}
165	Across all dual-critic configurations ($n{=}7$), critics trained on shared RSSM features converge ($\rho > 0.999$), with pessimism at 3--4\% of the independent-critic bound (\S\ref{sec:dual_critic_method}).
166	
167	\paragraph{$2{\times}2$ factorial: recovery vs.\ prevention.}
168	Min drives recovery: narrow dual-critic (${\sim}1.2$M) recovers fully (${\sim}16$--$19\times$ recovery ratio) despite $2\times$ fewer parameters. Prevention requires min $+$ capacity: wide dual-critic maintains $\nve \leq 1.4\times$ (3/3 prevention, 1/3 non-CIVO collapse). Cross-environment transfer is limited (Appendix~\ref{sec:dual_critic_appendix}).
169	
170	%% ============================================================
171	%% Online CIVO Detection
172	%% ============================================================
173	
174	\subsection{Online CIVO Detection}
175	\label{sec:detector}
176	
177	We derive a lightweight online detector monitoring the value-to-evaluation ratio for superlinear growth. Applied to 14 runs across three environments, it achieves TPR${=}5/5$, FPR${=}2/9$, with predictive alerts in 2/5 cases (Appendix~\ref{sec:detector_appendix}).
178

0.97$\\times$ & Healthy \\\\\n008a & Unexposed emb & exp$^a$ & OFF & 15 & 777.7 @105K & 0.93$\\times$ & Healthy \\\\\n015b & Frozen emb & frz$^b$ & ON & 8 & 204.2 @105K & 1.45$\\times$ & Grad.\\ starvation \\\\\n022b & Frozen emb + Gumbel gate & frz$^b$ & ON & 8 & 300.7 @105K & 0.63$\\times$ & No CIVO \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\textit{Cross-environment validation}} \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n010b & Cheetah Run & trn & ON & 8 & 51.7 @105K & 8.27$\\times$ & Collapsed \\\\\n019b & PickCube-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 26.64 @200K & 3.78$\\times$$^c$ & Transient onset \\\\\n049 & StackCube-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 34.9 @212K & 5.05$\\times$$^d$ & Env.\\ transient \\\\\n050 & PegInsertionSide-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 20.86 @212K & 10.05$\\times$$^e$ & Env.\\ dynamics$^e$ \\\\\n046 & Cheetah Run & frz$^b$ & ON & 8 & 150.5 @215K & 1.35$\\times$ & No CIVO \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize\ntrn = trained ($d{=}32$, subsample ON);\nexp$^a$ = trained, subsample OFF;\nfrz$^b$ = frozen ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False});\n--- = removed ($d{=}0$).\\\\\n$^c$ NVE normalized against exp\\_019a\\_v2 (vanilla DreamerV3 on PickCube-v1).\\\\\n$^d$ StackCube transient: frozen control (exp\\_051) exhibits identical spike (peak $4.30\\times$ at 42.5K, self-correcting by 82.5K), indicating environment-specific phenomenon unrelated to $\\dt$-embedding training.\\\\\n$^e$ Seed~0 trained peaks at $10.05\\times$ (82.5K) with delayed self-correction; seed~1 trained (exp\\_055) peaks at $3.08\\times$ (92.5K), final $0.50\\times$; seed~1 frozen (exp\\_056) peaks at $11.32\\times$ (82.5K), final $1.19\\times$. Environment-specific stochastic dynamics: frozen final exceeds trained final.\\par}\n\\end{table*}\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F1: All dt>1 degrade\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F1: All Variable-Rate Configurations Degrade}\n\\label{sec:f1}\n\nEvery configuration that introduces $\\dt > 1$ imagination degrades relative to the $\\dt{=}1$ baseline (Figure~\\ref{fig:learning_curves}). The baseline reaches 915.7 at 105K; fixed $\\dt{=}2$ (006) peaks at 485.3 before collapsing to 132.9 ($14\\%$ of baseline); fixed $\\dt{=}4$ (007) never exceeds 176.9 ($19\\%$); the learned gate (005) collapses to 98.8 ($11\\%$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_learning_curves.pdf}\n\\caption{Learning curves. The baseline (black) reaches 915.7; all variable-rate configurations degrade.}\n\\label{fig:learning_curves}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F2: Trained dt_embedding → nve > 1.5x\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F2: Trained Temporal Embedding Precedes Value Overestimation}\n\\label{sec:f2}\n\nThe factorial design isolates trained $\\dt$-embedding as the strongest predictor. The \\emph{primary} comparison---008d (trained, subsample ON) vs.\\ 008f (no embedding, subsample ON)---differs only in embedding training, yielding $\\nve = 4.12\\times$ (eval 82.8) vs.\\ $\\nve \\leq 0.97\\times$ (eval 503.0). \\emph{Secondary} comparisons against 008a/008c (unexposed embedding) co-vary subsample status and horizon alongside the embedding factor. The $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 26 of 29 experimental configurations across two environments (AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, Table~\\ref{tab:nve_loeo}; Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\\pm$ seed range). Trained $\\dt$-embedding (red, Walker Walk, $n{=}3$ seeds plotted; seed~4 data in Table~\\ref{tab:complete_results}) exceeds the $1.5\\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}\n\\label{fig:nve_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe trained $\\dt$-embedding precedes CIVO in all five Walker Walk seeds (peak NVE: $4.72\\times$, $4.62\\times$, $4.12\\times$, $1.62\\times$, $1.53\\times$), universally degrading performance to below 30\\% of baseline (IQM $175.2$, mean $170.6$, $n{=}5$; \\citealt{agarwal2021precipice}). Downstream trajectories diverge: two seeds undergo full collapse, one exhibits late-onset collapse still declining at 105K, and two self-correct (seeds 2 and 5). Combined with Cheetah Run (\\S\\ref{sec:f6}), CIVO onset is universal across all seven tested seeds ($n{=}7$, two environments) with 5/7 progressing toward collapse, 2/7 self-correcting (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}\n\\label{sec:f3f5_summary}\n\nTrained $\\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure are \\emph{jointly sufficient in tested configurations} for CIVO. Neither factor alone precedes pathological overestimation: trained embedding without multi-rate exposure (008a, 3 seeds, peak NVE $0.83$--$0.93\\times$) and multi-rate exposure without embedding (008f, 3 seeds, peak NVE $\\leq 1.41\\times$) both remain below $1.5\\times$. Cross-environment replication on Cheetah Run confirms generalization. Freezing the embedding (\\texttt{requires\\_grad=False}) dramatically reduces severity (NVE $0.19$--$1.88\\times$ vs.\\ trained $1.53$--$4.72\\times$; 2/4 seeds below threshold, 2/4 transient self-correcting spikes; Table~\\ref{tab:three_way_emb}); removing the embedding (008f) also avoids CIVO. Partial gradient blocking (exp\\_044) does not prevent onset (peak NVE $4.45\\times$) but permits delayed self-correction (final eval 538, 59\\% baseline; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), confirming that \\emph{any} gradient pathway updating $e_{\\dt}$ suffices to induce CIVO. Degradation scales monotonically with $\\dt$ ($-47\\%$ at $\\dt{=}2$, $-81\\%$ at $\\dt{=}4$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dt_amplification}).\n\n\\paragraph{ManiSkill manipulation tasks.}\nNone of three ManiSkill environments exhibit $\\dt$-embedding-dependent CIVO. StackCube-v1 value-ratio spikes are environment-specific: trained (049/052, NVE $5.05$/$4.67\\times$) and frozen (051, $4.30\\times$) match, all self-correcting by 82.5K. PegInsertionSide-v1 exhibits high-amplitude stochastic dynamics ($4$--$11\\times$) whose trained/frozen ordering is seed-dependent: seed~0 trained peaks higher ($10.05\\times$ vs.\\ frozen $1.82\\times$), but seed~1 reverses (frozen peak $11.32\\times$ vs.\\ trained peak $3.08\\times$). PickCube-v1 frozen control (exp\\_054, peak $8.81\\times$ at 62.5K, self-correcting) confirms the same pattern. CIVO is locomotion-specific in DreamerV3; the frozen-embedding diagnostic correctly identifies these transients as non-CIVO.\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F6--F7: Interventions and cross-environment\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F6--F7: Interventions and Multiple Pathways}\n\\label{sec:f6}\n\n\\paragraph{F6: Trained embedding creates multiple vulnerability pathways.}\nExp\\_008e\\_v3 restricts imagination to $\\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding. Two of four seeds collapse (50\\%): Pathway~A (CIVO, $\\nve = 4.02\\times$) and preliminary Pathway~B (entropy \\emph{increase}, $\\nve = 3.78\\times$; 2 seeds only, requires confirmation; Appendix~\\ref{sec:pathways}). Pathway~B produces one of three non-CIVO false positives ($n{=}29$).\n\\paragraph{F7: Full-pathway gradient stopping shows mixed results.}\nFull-SGS (exp\\_018) detaches all non-KL gradients from $e_{\\dt}$. Seed~1 shows partial mitigation (eval 448.3, NVE $1.66\\times$); seed~2 collapses (0/2 elimination; Appendix~\\ref{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah (010b, NVE $8.27$--$108.52\\times$) confirms CIVO generalization; full-SGS yields 0/3 elimination. Removing the embedding eliminates CIVO on Cheetah (024b, NVE $< 0.2\\times$).\n\n\\paragraph{Targeted obs-decoder-only SGS confirms pathway redundancy.}\nObs-decoder-only SGS (exp\\_025/026) achieves 1/6 CIVO prevention (1/4 Walker, 0/2 Cheetah; Table~\\ref{tab:intervention_hierarchy}), not meaningfully different from full-pathway SGS (0/3). CIVO operates through redundant gradient channels: blocking any single pathway shifts co-adaptation to the remaining ones (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Intervention hierarchy. Prevention = peak NVE $< 1.5\\times$ (35K+). No level reliably eliminates CIVO across environments.}\n\\label{tab:intervention_hierarchy}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Level} & \\textbf{Intervention} & \\textbf{Seeds} & \\textbf{Prev.} & \\textbf{Rate} \\\\\n\\midrule\n--- & Untreated & 7 & 0/7 & 0\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nGradient & Reward-only SGS & 2 & 0/2 & 0\\% \\\\\nGradient & Full-pathway SGS & 3 & 0/3 & 0\\% \\\\\nGradient & Obs-decoder SGS & 6 & 1/6 & 17\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nOutput & RAVE ($c{=}1.5{,}2.0$) & 2 & 0/2 & 0\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nRepr. & TCN ($\\sigma{=}0.1$) & 1 & 1/1$^\\ddagger$ & 100\\% \\\\\nRepr. & TCN ($\\sigma{=}0.2$) & 6$^\\dagger$ & 2/6 & 33\\% \\\\\nRepr. & TCN ($\\sigma{=}0.5$) & 3 & 0/3 & 0\\% \\\\\nRepr. & TCN ($\\sigma{=}1.0$) & 1 & 1/1$^\\S$ & 100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nArch. & Wide single & 2 & 0/2 & 0\\% \\\\\nArch. & Narrow dual (min) & 2 & 0/2 & 0\\% \\\\\nArch. & Wide dual (min) & 4 & 3/4$^\\|$ & 75\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n--- & Embedding frozen & 7 & 5/7$^\\#$ & 71\\% \\\\\n--- & Embedding removed & 5 & 5/5 & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Walker 2/4 (s2 NVE $2.11\\times$; s3 late-onset CIVO, NVE $3.04\\times$ at 105K); Cheetah 0/1, PickCube 0/1.\n$^\\ddagger$Borderline (NVE${=}1.02\\times$, eval 55\\%).\n$^\\S$CIVO prevented but progressive policy erosion (peak 116\\%, final 15\\% baseline).\n$^\\|$Walker 3/3 ($\\nve \\leq 1.4\\times$; 1/3 non-CIVO eval collapse), Cheetah 0/1.\\\\\n$^\\#$Walker 5 + Cheetah 2. Exp\\_047 (NVE $1.71\\times$) and Exp\\_057 (NVE $1.88\\times$): both transient, self-correcting within 20K steps; remaining 5 seeds: NVE $0.19$--$1.45\\times$.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% Forward-pass interventions\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{Forward-Pass Interventions}\n\\label{sec:forward_results}\n\nWe test two forward-pass alternatives (Table~\\ref{tab:intervention_hierarchy}). \\textbf{RAVE} (value-level capping) fails: the EMA anchor tracks biased values during CIVO onset. \\textbf{TCN} (representation-level noise) suppresses CIVO in 2 of 4 Walker seeds at $\\sigma{=}0.2$ (NVE $< 1.0\\times$, eval 45--91\\%), but remaining seeds show runaway escalation, suggesting TCN gates rather than attenuates CIVO. Cross-environment transfer fails: Cheetah effective dose ($\\sigma{=}1.0$) causes policy erosion to 15\\% baseline; PickCube 0/1 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tcn_cross_env}). TCN is a \\emph{diagnostic probe}, not a deployable mitigation.\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% Architecture-Level Ablation\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{Architecture-Level Ablation}\n\\label{sec:arch_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Critic convergence in pure imagination.}\nAcross all dual-critic configurations ($n{=}7$), critics trained on shared RSSM features converge ($\\rho > 0.999$), with pessimism at 3--4\\% of the independent-critic bound (\\S\\ref{sec:dual_critic_method}).\n\n\\paragraph{$2{\\times}2$ factorial: recovery vs.\\ prevention.}\nMin drives recovery: narrow dual-critic (${\\sim}1.2$M) recovers fully (${\\sim}16$--$19\\times$ recovery ratio) despite $2\\times$ fewer parameters. Prevention requires min $+$ capacity: wide dual-critic maintains $\\nve \\leq 1.4\\times$ (3/3 prevention, 1/3 non-CIVO collapse). Cross-environment transfer is limited (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dual_critic_appendix}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% Online CIVO Detection\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{Online CIVO Detection}\n\\label{sec:detector}\n\nWe derive a lightweight online detector monitoring the value-to-evaluation ratio for superlinear growth. Applied to 14 runs across three environments, it achieves TPR${=}5/5$, FPR${=}2/9$, with predictive alerts in 2/5 cases (Appendix~\\ref{sec:detector_appendix}).\n",
      "numLines": 178,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 178
    }
  }
}

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We identify trained $\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of CIVO in DreamerV3 (26/29, AUROC${=}0.953$; LOEO cross-validated). No tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO; in pure imagination, dual critics converge ($\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective. The feedback architecture---MPC re-planning versus imagination-based bootstrapping---is the structural factor.
5	
6	\paragraph{Limitations.}
7	Our analysis covers proprioceptive locomotion (DMC) and simulated manipulation (ManiSkill) but does not test pixel-based observations, Atari benchmarks, or real-robot deployments where sensor noise and actuation latency introduce additional confounds.
8	All experiments use DreamerV3's RSSM; whether CIVO manifests in other imagination-based architectures (e.g., IRIS \citep{micheli2023iris} or latent-space planners beyond TD-MPC2) remains open.
9	We characterize CIVO under fixed-$\dt$ subsampling; learned temporal gates warrant separate study.
10	Statistical power with 4 seeds per configuration is limited \citep{colas2018seeds}; the NVE threshold (LOEO AUROC${=}0.981$) may require recalibration for different reward scales.
11	Code: upon acceptance.
12	\label{sec:limitations}
13	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Azw2mRBQfScxPjTmBNfrN4`</summary>

```text
225	
226	For Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), perfect classification is achievable at $\nve \geq 3.9\times$, but with only $n_{\text{pos}} = 3$ the statistical power remains limited (accuracy 95\% CI: [69.2\%, 100\%]).
227	
228	\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}
229	\label{sec:roc_analysis}
230	
231	The original $n{=}16$ analysis (Tables~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}29$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).
232	
233	\begin{figure}[h]
234	\centering
235	\includegraphics[width=0.7\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}
236	\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}29$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}
237	\label{fig:nve_roc}
238	\end{figure}
239	
240	We label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.
241	
242	The three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\_008e\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\times$) lacks $\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\% of baseline.
243	
244	\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}
245	\label{sec:nve_robustness}
246	
247	To assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).
248	
249	\begin{table}[h]
250	\centering
251	\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}
252	\label{tab:baseline_seeds}
253	\footnotesize
254	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
255	\toprule
256	\textbf{Seed} & \textbf{Eval@105K} & \textbf{Eval@505K} & \textbf{ve@65K} & \textbf{ve@105K} \\
257	\midrule
258	s1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\
259	s2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\
260	s3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\
261	s4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\
262	\bottomrule
263	\end{tabular}
264	\end{table}
265	
266	Seed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\times$) to fall below the $1.5\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.
267	
268	Single-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\%): the boundary case exp\_009b flips from $\nve = 1.62\times$ (s1) to $1.23\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\times \to 4.52\times$; 008d: $4.12\times \to 3.54\times$). The $1.5\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\% of the threshold.
269	
270	\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).
271	
272	\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 10$ experiments, Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals remain wide (e.g., 90.0\% accuracy has 95\% CI [55.5\%, 99.7\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds. Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full} reports the original $n{=}16$ evaluation set spanning both environments. The expanded analysis ($n{=}29$, including SGS variants, RAVE, and TCN configurations) yields AUROC${=}0.953$ (Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc}, in-sample, post-hoc). The original $n{=}16$ core analysis yields AUROC${=}0.969$; the expanded $n{=}29$ set includes SGS, RAVE, TCN, and dual-critic configurations. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.
273	
274	\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}
275	\label{sec:nve_loeo}
276	
277	To assess whether $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}13$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\theta{=}1.5\times$ on the held-out environment.
278	
279	\begin{table}[h]
280	\centering
281	\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\theta=1.5\times$.
282	For each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \emph{not} used
283	to select $\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;
284	$\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}
285	\label{tab:nve_loeo}
286	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
287	\toprule
288	Held-out & $N_{\text{train}}$ & $N_{\text{test}}$ & $\theta_{\text{opt}}$ range & Test Accuracy & Test AUROC \\
289	\midrule
290	PickCube & 18 & 2 & $(1.45,\, 1.53]$ & 2/2 (1.000) & 1.000 \\
291	Cheetah  & 15 & 5 & $(1.45,\, 1.53]$ & 5/5 (1.000) & 1.000 \\
292	Walker   &  7 & 13 & $(1.00,\, 3.78)$ & 12/13 (0.923) & 0.944 \\
293	\midrule
294	\textbf{Mean} & — & — & — & \textbf{0.974} & \textbf{0.981} \\
295	\bottomrule
296	\end{tabular}
297	\end{table}
298	
299	The threshold $\theta{=}1.5\times$ falls within the optimal accuracy range for all three training folds: $(1.45, 1.53]$ when Walker data is available, and the wider $(1.00, 3.78)$ when training on Cheetah$+$PickCube only (where CIVO and non-CIVO NVE values are well-separated). The single cross-validation error is exp\_008e\_v3 seed~2 (Walker), an IRD collapse mechanistically distinct from CIVO (\S\ref{sec:roc_analysis}). Mean held-out accuracy is 0.974 and mean AUROC is 0.981, confirming that $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes across environments without retuning. PickCube fold confidence intervals remain wide (Clopper-Pearson 95\% CI: $[0.158, 1.000]$) due to $N_{\text{test}}{=}2$.
300	
301	% --- APPENDIX B: Factorial Ablation Details ---
302	
303	\section{Factorial Ablation Details}
304	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
305	
306	This appendix provides the full factorial ablation results summarized in \S\ref{sec:f3f5_summary} of the main text.
307	
308	\paragraph{Embedding gradient analysis.}
309	Table~\ref{tab:three_way_emb} isolates embedding \emph{trainability} as the necessary condition for CIVO by comparing four gradient conditions under otherwise identical configurations ($H{=}8$, subsample ON, $\dt{=}2$ imagination).
310	
311	\begin{table}[h]
312	\centering

We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}29$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}29$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample, post-hoc). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}\n\\label{fig:nve_roc}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.\n\nThe three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \\emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \\emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\\times$) lacks $\\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\\% of baseline.\n\n\\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nve_robustness}\n\nTo assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}\n\\label{tab:baseline_seeds}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Eval@105K} & \\textbf{Eval@505K} & \\textbf{ve@65K} & \\textbf{ve@105K} \\\\\n\\midrule\ns1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\\\\ns2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\\\\ns3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\\\\ns4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\\times$) to fall below the $1.5\\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.\n\nSingle-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\\%): the boundary case exp\\_009b flips from $\\nve = 1.62\\times$ (s1) to $1.23\\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\\times \\to 4.52\\times$; 008d: $4.12\\times \\to 3.54\\times$). The $1.5\\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\\% of the threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 10$ experiments, Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals remain wide (e.g., 90.0\\% accuracy has 95\\% CI [55.5\\%, 99.7\\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds. Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full} reports the original $n{=}16$ evaluation set spanning both environments. The expanded analysis ($n{=}29$, including SGS variants, RAVE, and TCN configurations) yields AUROC${=}0.953$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc}, in-sample, post-hoc). The original $n{=}16$ core analysis yields AUROC${=}0.969$; the expanded $n{=}29$ set includes SGS, RAVE, TCN, and dual-critic configurations. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}\n\\label{sec:nve_loeo}\n\nTo assess whether $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}13$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ on the held-out environment.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\\theta=1.5\\times$.\nFor each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \\emph{not} used\nto select $\\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;\n$\\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}\n\\label{tab:nve_loeo}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHeld-out & $N_{\\text{train}}$ & $N_{\\text{test}}$ & $\\theta_{\\text{opt}}$ range & Test Accuracy & Test AUROC \\\\\n\\midrule\nPickCube & 18 & 2 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 2/2 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nCheetah  & 15 & 5 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 5/5 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nWalker   &  7 & 13 & $(1.00,\\, 3.78)$ & 12/13 (0.923) & 0.944 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Mean} & — & — & — & \\textbf{0.974} & \\textbf{0.981} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe threshold $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ falls within the optimal accuracy range for all three training folds: $(1.45, 1.53]$ when Walker data is available, and the wider $(1.00, 3.78)$ when training on Cheetah$+$PickCube only (where CIVO and non-CIVO NVE values are well-separated). The single cross-validation error is exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2 (Walker), an IRD collapse mechanistically distinct from CIVO (\\S\\ref{sec:roc_analysis}). Mean held-out accuracy is 0.974 and mean AUROC is 0.981, confirming that $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes across environments without retuning. ```text
475	The RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO (Figure~\ref{fig:civo_mechanism}).  The trained $\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.
476	
477	This asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\nve$ above $1.5\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.
478	
479	A random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\times$ higher mean reward than $\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\approx 36\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\approx 64\%$ to actor exploitation, which confirms that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.
480	
481	This mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.
482	
483	\paragraph{Signed reward error analysis.}
484	To test whether CIVO arises from one-step reward overprediction, we compute signed error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions (Table~\ref{tab:signed_error}). The trained-embedding model (008d) shows \emph{negative} bias at $\dt{=}2$ (mean signed error $-0.599$, only 8\% of samples overpredicted), ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the mechanism. The baseline is well-calibrated (mean SE $\approx 0$), confirming methodological soundness. Value overestimation therefore occurs during \emph{imagination-level} prior drift: the RSSM prior (unconditioned on observations) generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\dt > 1$ (Stage~1 above, $1.68\times$ ratio under random policy), and the critic accumulates these into overestimated returns. The one-step reward prediction is negatively biased when conditioned on real observations, but this posterior correction is unavailable during imagination. This also distinguishes CIVO from generic compounding error, which would produce \emph{positive} signed error accumulating with horizon; the negative observation-level bias confirms that CIVO is a prior-drift phenomenon, not a reward-prediction artifact.
```

```text
640	
641	\subsection{Why MPC Is Immune}
642	\label{sec:mpc_immune}
643	
644	\citet{nhu2025tawm} show that $\dt$-conditioning benefits TD-MPC2, raising the question of why it fails in DreamerV3.  The critical difference is that MPC re-plans from real states at every step, so imagination errors do not accumulate into the training signal because there is no critic bootstrapping from imagined trajectories.  In DreamerV3, the actor-critic trains entirely within imagination, and any systematic reward-prediction bias at $\dt > 1$ is amplified through the closed actor-critic loop.
645	
646	\subsection{The \texorpdfstring{$\dt$}{dt}-Embedding Dilemma}
647	\label{sec:embedding_dilemma}
648	
649	The factorial results reveal a fundamental tension: removing the $\dt$-embedding (008f) eliminates CIVO but at a $13\times$ cost to reward prediction quality ($0.7469$ vs.\ $0.0568$ MAE), as the world model cannot distinguish temporal scales.  Restricting imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding (008e\_v3) avoids collapse in 2 of 4 seeds (50\%), while the remaining two collapse via distinct mechanisms: Pathway~A (CIVO-driven, entropy decrease; s3, NVE $4.02\times$) and Pathway~B (entropy-driven, entropy increase; s2, NVE $3.78\times$; \S\ref{sec:f6}).  Neither approach alone provides a complete solution.
650	
651	\subsection{Multiple Vulnerability Pathways}
652	\label{sec:pathways}
653	
654	The two collapse patterns identified in \S\ref{sec:f6}---CIVO (Pathway~A, value-driven, entropy decreasing) and a tentative second mode (Pathway~B, eval-driven, entropy increasing)---have distinct diagnostic signatures. Pathway~B is observed in only two seeds (008e\_v3\_s2 and 038\_s2); we present it as preliminary evidence that the trained $\dt$-embedding's learned features may affect both reward prediction across temporal scales (Pathway~A) and the representation space in which the actor operates (Pathway~B), though additional data is needed to confirm this as a distinct mechanism.  This explains why $\nve$ is sensitive but not perfectly specific: Pathway~B's late evaluation decline inflates $\nve$ through the same arithmetic as CIVO, yielding the sole false positive ($3.78\times$ for 008e\_v3 s2).  Distinguishing the two requires examining the temporal profile and entropy direction of collapse (Figure~\ref{fig:multiple_pathways}).
```

</details>

Factorial diagnostics on two continuous-control tasks isolate trained temporal embedding with multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor of this failure---\\emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO; 26/29, AUROC${=}0.953$, LOEO cross-validated). The RSSM develops a $2.78\\times$ reward prediction gap at $\\Delta t{>}1$ that the critic internalizes as optimistic values; the contrast with MPC identifies feedback architecture as the structural factor. No tested intervention across four levels (gradient, output, representation, architecture) reliably eliminates CIVO: dual critics converge in pure imagination ($\\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective, though min $+$ increased capacity enables recovery. A lightweight NVE detector achieves 100\\% sensitivity.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWorld-model reinforcement learning enables sample-efficient learning directly on physical hardware \\citep{wu2023daydreamer, hansen2024tdmpc2}. Real robotic tasks demand temporal abstraction: manipulation requires fast reactive grasping alongside slow approach planning, locomotion involves gait phases at different frequencies, and sim-to-real transfer must handle $\\dt$ mismatches between simulation and hardware. TAWM \\citep{nhu2025tawm} shows that conditioning a world model on step size $\\dt$ yields consistent gains within TD-MPC2's MPC framework \\citep{hansen2024tdmpc2}. Can the same $\\dt$-conditioning bring temporal abstraction to imagination-based actor-critic methods like DreamerV3 \\citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, where policies are trained entirely in imagined rollouts?\n\nWe find that it cannot. Every variable-rate configuration degrades performance catastrophically (14--19\\% of baseline). Through a factorial analysis on Walker Walk \\citep{tassa2018dmcontrol}, we isolate the combination of a \\emph{trained} $\\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as the strongest predictor. This combination enables the RSSM reward head to develop a $2.78\\times$ prediction gap between $\\dt{=}1$ and $\\dt{=}2$, which the critic internalizes as optimistic value estimates, a failure mode we term \\emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO), analogous to delusional bias \\citep{lu2018nondelusional} but model-induced rather than policy-class-induced. A normalized value-to-eval (NVE) threshold of $\\geq 1.5\\times$ achieves 26/29 classification ($n{=}29$, two environments, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, Appendix~Table~\\ref{tab:nve_loeo}).\nGradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude does not predict causal importance: the reward head contributes 38--49\\% of $\\nabla_\\theta e_{\\dt}$ yet reward-only stopping fails, while the observation decoder (16--22\\%) carries the critical signal. A four-level intervention hierarchy (gradient, output, representation, architecture) shows that no tested intervention reliably eliminates CIVO across environments. In pure-imagination training, dual critics converge to identical value functions ($\\rho > 0.999$), rendering Double Q pessimism ineffective; the min operation nonetheless enables CIVO recovery, while prevention requires min $+$ increased capacity. These findings are directly relevant to robotics: as world-model RL moves toward real systems with variable-rate sensing and multi-timescale control, understanding when temporal conditioning fails, and why standard countermeasures cannot fix it, is essential for safe deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Contributions.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Failure mode identification.} We identify CIVO as specific to imagination-based actor-critic with temporal conditioning and isolate trained $\\dt$-embedding $+$ multi-rate exposure as jointly sufficient in tested configurations (7/7 seeds, two locomotion environments, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated). Cross-environment validation spanning locomotion and manipulation (ManiSkill) establishes that CIVO is locomotion-specific; frozen-embedding controls on three manipulation tasks identify value-ratio transients as environment-driven, demonstrating the diagnostic's specificity on non-CIVO dynamics.\n    \\item \\textbf{Architectural boundary.} $\\dt$-conditioning succeeds in TD-MPC2's MPC re-planning but fails under imagination-based bootstrapping, where model errors compound into biased value targets with no real-data anchor.\n    \\item \\textbf{Four-level intervention hierarchy.} Across gradient, output, representation, and architecture levels, no intervention reliably eliminates CIVO across environments. Gradient magnitude does not predict causal importance (reward head: 38--49\\%, reward-only stopping fails). In pure-imagination training, dual critics converge ($\\rho > 0.999$), eliminating Double Q pessimism, though the min operation enables recovery through a different mechanism.\n    \\item \\textbf{Online detection.} A lightweight NVE-based detector achieves 100\\% sensitivity (5/5) with predictive warning in 2/5 cases.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
