Includes Walker Walk (14 configurations) and Cheetah Run (2 configurations).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nThreshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\geq 1.0\\times$ & 8 & 2 & 0 & 6 & 87.5\\% \\; [61.7, 98.4] & 80.0\\% & 100\\% & 88.9\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.2\\times$ & 8 & 1 & 0 & 7 & 93.8\\% \\; [69.8, 99.8] & 88.9\\% & 100\\% & 94.1\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.5\\times$ & 8 & 1 & 0 & 7 & 93.8\\% \\; [69.8, 99.8] & 88.9\\% & 100\\% & 94.1\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.7\\times$ & 6 & 1 & 2 & 7 & 81.3\\% \\; [54.4, 96.0] & 85.7\\% & 75.0\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 2.0\\times$ & 6 & 1 & 2 & 7 & 81.3\\% \\; [54.4, 96.0] & 85.7\\% & 75.0\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 3.9\\times$ & 6 & 0 & 2 & 8 & 87.5\\% \\; [61.7, 98.4] & 100\\% & 75.0\\% & 85.7\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Threshold Recommendation}\n\nFor the main diagnostic (Task~B2), we recommend $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ as the operating threshold for three reasons:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Maximum recall.} The $1.5\\times$ threshold achieves 100\\% recall, detecting all four CIVO cases including the transient case (009b, $\\nve = 1.62\\times$). Raising the threshold to $1.7\\times$ loses 009b, dropping recall to 75.0\\%.\n\\item \\textbf{Best F1.} At $1.5\\times$, F1 = 88.9\\%, the highest among all tested thresholds for B2. The single false positive (008e\\_v3\\_s2) is mechanistically identifiable as IRD rather than CIVO.\n\\item \\textbf{Sensitivity--specificity tradeoff.} Lower thresholds ($1.0\\times$) add false positives without gaining true positives. Higher thresholds ($\\geq 1.7\\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\\_v3\\_s2 false positive (which requires $\\tau > 3.78$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nFor Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), perfect classification is achievable at $\\nve \\geq 3.9\\times$, but with only $n_{\\text{pos}} = 3$ the statistical power remains limited (accuracy 95\\% CI: [69.2\\%, 100\\%]).\n\n\\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}\n\\label{sec:roc_analysis}\n\nThe original $n{=}16$ analysis (Tables~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}29$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}29$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.953$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}\n\\label{fig:nve_roc}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.\n\nThe three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \\emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \\emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\\times$) lacks $\\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\\% of baseline.\n\n\\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nve_robustness}\n\nTo assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}\n\\label{tab:baseline_seeds}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Eval@105K} & \\textbf{Eval@505K} & \\textbf{ve@65K} & \\textbf{ve@105K} \\\\\n\\midrule\ns1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\\\\ns2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\\\\ns3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\\\\ns4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\\times$) to fall below the $1.5\\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.\n\nSingle-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\\%): the boundary case exp\\_009b flips from $\\nve = 1.62\\times$ (s1) to $1.23\\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\\times \\to 4.52\\times$; 008d: $4.12\\times \\to 3.54\\times$). The $1.5\\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\\% of the threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 10$ experiments, Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals remain wide (e.g., 90.0\\% accuracy has 95\\% CI [55.5\\%, 99.7\\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds. Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full} reports the original $n{=}16$ evaluation set spanning both environments. The expanded analysis ($n{=}29$, including SGS variants, RAVE, and TCN configurations) yields AUROC${=}0.953$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc}, in-sample, post-hoc). The original $n{=}16$ core analysis yields AUROC${=}0.969$; the expanded $n{=}29$ set includes SGS, RAVE, TCN, and dual-critic configurations. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}\n\\label{sec:nve_loeo}\n\nTo assess whether $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}13$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ on the held-out environment.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\\theta=1.5\\times$.\nFor each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \\emph{not} used\nto select $\\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;\n$\\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}\n\\label{tab:nve_loeo}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHeld-out & $N_{\\text{train}}$ & $N_{\\text{test}}$ & $\\theta_{\\text{opt}}$ range & Test Accuracy & Test AUROC \\\\\n\\midrule\nPickCube & 18 & 2 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 2/2 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nCheetah  & 15 & 5 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 5/5 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nWalker   &  7 & 13 & $(1.00,\\, 3.78)$ & 12/13 (0.923) & 0.944 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Mean} & — & — & — & \\textbf{0.974} & \\textbf{0.981} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe threshold $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ falls within the optimal accuracy range for all three training folds: $(1.45, 1.53]$ when Walker data is available, and the wider $(1.00, 3.78)$ when training on Cheetah$+$PickCube only (where CIVO and non-CIVO NVE values are well-separated). The single cross-validation error is exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2 (Walker), an IRD collapse mechanistically distinct from CIVO (\\S\\ref{sec:roc_analysis}). Mean held-out accuracy is 0.974 and mean AUROC is 0.981, confirming that $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes across environments without retuning. PickCube fold confidence intervals remain wide (Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI: $[0.158, 1.000]$) due to $N_{\\text{test}}{=}2$.\n\n% --- APPENDIX B: Factorial Ablation Details ---\n\n\\section{Factorial Ablation Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nThis appendix provides the full factorial ablation results summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:f3f5_summary} of the main text.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding gradient analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:three_way_emb} isolates embedding \\emph{trainability} as the necessary condition for CIVO by comparing four gradient conditions under otherwise identical configurations ($H{=}8$, subsample ON, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$\\dt$-embedding gradient conditions on Walker Walk. Any gradient pathway updating $e_{\\dt}$ is sufficient to induce CIVO; freezing or removing the embedding dramatically reduces severity.}\n\\label{tab:three_way_emb}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccl}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Grad flow & Exp(s) & NVE peak & Eval & CIVO \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained & all & 008d, 059 & $1.53$--$4.72\\times$ & 64--268 & 5/5 \\\\\nReward detach & obs+KL only & 044 & $4.45\\times$ & 44$\\to$538$^\\dagger$ & 1/1 \\\\\nFull SGS & KL only & 018 & $1.66\\times$ & 448 & 1/1$^*$ \\\\\nFrozen & none & 042, 015b, 047, 057 & $0.19$--$1.88\\times$$^\\ddagger$ & 204--612 & 2/4 \\\\\nRemoved & N/A & 008f & $0.97$--$1.41\\times$ & 307--503 & 0/3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^*$Full-SGS seed~1 (NVE $1.66\\times$); seed~2 collapsed. $^\\dagger$Self-corrected at ${\\sim}175$K (eval 44$\\to$538, 59\\% baseline; 224K run). $^\\ddagger$Exp\\_047 (NVE $1.71\\times$) and Exp\\_057 (NVE $1.88\\times$): both transient, self-correcting within 20K steps.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe reward-detach condition (exp\\_044, \\texttt{reward\\_dt\\_emb\\_detach=True}) uses a backward hook to block reward-head gradients from reaching $e_{\\dt}$, complementing the framework-level Reward SGS (exp\\_016e). Both yield the same directional result---CIVO onset not prevented---ruling out implementation artifacts. Exp\\_044 exhibits delayed CIVO onset (${\\sim}95$K vs.\\ typical $25$--$35$K), consistent with reduced but non-zero gradient pressure on $e_{\\dt}$. Over a full 224K run, exp\\_044 self-corrects: eval recovers from 44 (95K) to 538 (215K, 59\\% baseline), but this recovery is incomplete compared to spontaneous recovery without gradient intervention (exp\\_045: 868, 95\\% baseline). The lower asymptote suggests that selectively disrupting gradient flow may create optimization difficulties beyond CIVO itself.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:factorial_design} summarizes the experimental configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Factorial design. Each row specifies one experimental configuration. \\textbf{Emb}: $\\dt$-embedding mode. \\textbf{Sub}: temporal subsampling. \\textbf{H}: imagination horizon. \\textbf{Imag $\\dt$}: temporal resolution used during imagination.}\n\\label{tab:factorial_design}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{ID} & \\textbf{Purpose} & \\textbf{Emb} & \\textbf{Sub} & \\textbf{H} & \\textbf{Imag $\\dt$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n001 & Reference baseline & --- & --- & 15 & 1 \\\\\n006 & Fixed $\\dt{=}2$ & trn & on & 15 & 2 \\\\\n007 & Fixed $\\dt{=}4$ & trn & on & 15 & 4 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008a & Unexposed emb ctrl & exp$^a$ & off & 15 & 2 \\\\\n008c & Horizon control & exp$^a$ & off & 8 & 2 \\\\\n008d & Collapse condition & trn & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n008e\\_v3 & $\\dt{=}1$ imag control & trn & on & 15 & 1 \\\\\n008f & Causal ablation & --- & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n015b & Frozen-emb ctrl & frz$^b$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n016e & Stop-grad (reward) & trn$^\\dagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n018 & Full-SGS & trn$^\\ddagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n005 & Learned gate & adaptive & off & 15 & learned \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize\ntrn = trained ($d{=}32$, exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ data via subsample ON);\nexp$^a$ = trained ($d{=}32$) but never exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ data (subsample OFF);\nfrz$^b$ = frozen random projection ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False});\n--- = removed ($d{=}0$).\\\\\n$^\\dagger$ \\texttt{reward\\_dt\\_emb\\_detach=True}; $^\\ddagger$ all non-KL gradients to \\texttt{dt\\_emb} detached.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{F3: Unexposed Temporal Embedding Suppresses Value Drift}\n\\label{sec:f3}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_factorial_grid.pdf}\n\\caption{Factorial isolation of CIVO trigger. Only the combination of trained $e_{\\dt}$ and multi-rate data (upper-left cell) produces pathological NVE. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{fig:factorial_grid}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe empirical finding that trained embedding \\emph{plus} multi-rate exposure jointly predict CIVO rests on two complementary controls that each remove one factor while holding the other constant.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence A: exposure is necessary (exp\\_008a).}\nExp\\_008a uses a trained embedding ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) that is never exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during world-model training (subsample OFF). Despite sharing the same embedding architecture as exp\\_008d, its NVE remains in the $0.83$--$0.93\\times$ range across 3 seeds (105K steps). Performance follows two V-shaped recovery cycles, reaching 777.7 at 105K (85\\% of baseline). The critical factor is not the embedding's capacity to represent temporal information, but its adaptation to multi-rate data that enables precise but inaccurate $\\dt{>}1$ state predictions within the RSSM.\n\nThis contrasts with exp\\_006 (trained $e_{\\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}15$), where NVE accelerates from $1.61\\times$ at 45K to $2.31\\times$ at 55K before catastrophic collapse to 132.9 at 65K. Both experiments use identical trained embeddings ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) and $H{=}15$, differing only in whether the world model sees $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during training. This confirms that multi-rate data exposure, not embedding architecture, is the operative variable.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence B: trainability is necessary (exp\\_015b).}\nExp\\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.42\\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \\rightarrow 1.26 \\rightarrow 1.45 \\rightarrow 0.65 \\rightarrow 1.05 \\rightarrow 0.47 \\rightarrow 0.39 \\rightarrow 0.42$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \\S\\ref{sec:pathways}), the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.\n\nThe contrast with exp\\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \\rightarrow 1.71 \\rightarrow 2.09 \\rightarrow 4.12$) and precedes full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\\times$ before self-correcting to $0.42\\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\\dt$-embedding must be able to \\emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.\n\nTogether, these two controls establish that in all tested configurations, CIVO occurs only with the \\emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run replicates the empirical pattern: trained $e_{\\dt}$ precedes CIVO in both tested seeds (exp\\_010b, 2 seeds, NVE $8.27$--$108.52\\times$, collapse to 51.7/0.83) while removing the embedding eliminates it (exp\\_024b, 2 seeds, NVE $<0.2\\times$, eval 96.5/77.3). Freezing the embedding on Cheetah (exp\\_046, NVE${=}1.35\\times$ vs.\\ trained $70.64\\times$, a $52\\times$ reduction) further confirms that embedding trainability, not architecture, drives CIVO. This extends the sufficiency finding from Walker Walk to a second locomotion environment with qualitatively different dynamics (6-joint vs.\\ 2-joint).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Causal isolation: $e_{\\dt}$ configuration $\\times$ multi-rate data exposure. The combination of trained embedding and multi-rate exposure is the sole predictor of CIVO in tested configurations. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{tab:causal_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n& Multi-rate data (\\textsc{on}) & Single-rate data (\\textsc{off}) \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008d: \\textbf{CIVO} & exp\\_008a: healthy \\\\\n & NVE 4.12$\\times$, collapse & NVE 0.93$\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFrozen $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_015b: grad.\\ starvation & --- \\\\\n & peak NVE 1.45$\\times$ (transient) & \\\\\n\\midrule\nRemoved $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008f: no CIVO & --- \\\\\n & NVE $\\leq$0.97$\\times$ & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOnly the upper-left cell (trained embedding with multi-rate exposure) precedes CIVO (Table~\\ref{tab:causal_matrix}). This 2$\\times$2 structure establishes that both factors are jointly sufficient in tested configurations.\n\n\\subsection{F4: Degradation Scales Monotonically with \\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}}\n\\label{sec:f4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig5_dt_amplification.pdf}\n\\caption{Peak evaluation return as a function of imagination $\\dt$. Performance degrades monotonically: $\\dt{=}1$ (915.7) $>$ $\\dt{=}2$ (485.3, $-47\\%$) $>$ $\\dt{=}4$ (176.9, $-81\\%$).}\n\\label{fig:dt_amplification}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe magnitude of degradation scales monotonically with the imagination step size (Figure~\\ref{fig:dt_amplification}). From the $\\dt{=}1$ baseline (915.7 at 105K), fixed $\\dt{=}2$ (exp\\_006) achieves a peak of only 485.3 ($-47\\%$), and fixed $\\dt{=}4$ (exp\\_007) peaks at 176.9 ($-81\\%$). This monotonic relationship is consistent with compounding model error: larger temporal gaps amplify the RSSM's prediction inaccuracy at each imagination step, and these errors accumulate across the rollout horizon.\n\n\\subsection{F5: Imagination Horizon and Subsampling Are Secondary Factors}\n\\label{sec:f5}\n\nNeither horizon length nor temporal subsampling precedes collapse without multi-rate data exposure: both trained-but-unexposed configurations (008a, 008c) maintain $\\nve < 0.93\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:f3}), while the trained-and-exposed counterpart (008d) collapses with $\\nve = 4.12\\times$.  The no-embedding configurations show cross-seed variance (008f: 503.0 vs.\\ 009c: 148.3) but neither exhibits $\\nve > 1.5\\times$; low evaluation alone does not indicate CIVO.\n\nThe $3.2\\times$ performance gap (008a: 777.7 vs.\\ 008c: 240.9) quantifies the horizon effect independently of CIVO, as both conditions maintain healthy NVE ($\\leq$0.93$\\times$).\n\n% --- APPENDIX C: Full Analysis & Discussion ---\n\n\\section{Full Analysis \\& Discussion}\n\\label{sec:appendix_analysis}\n\nThis appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.\n\n\\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:civo_mechanism}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_mechanism_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO mechanism diagram. The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables asymmetric reward prediction fidelity, initiating a feedback loop: biased RSSM rewards $\\to$ optimistic critic values $\\to$ actor exploitation $\\to$ reinforced state-distribution skew.}\n\\label{fig:civo_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\\dt{=}1$ vs.\\ $\\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}\n\\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{obs\\_MSE}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{reward\\_MAE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\\times$ \\\\\n008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\\times$ \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO (Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_mechanism}).  The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.\n\nThis asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\\nve$ above $1.5\\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.\n\nA random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \\textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\\times$ higher mean reward than $\\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \\textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\\approx 36\\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\\approx 64\\%$ to actor exploitation, which confirms that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.\n\nThis mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \\citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Signed reward error analysis.}\nTo test whether CIVO arises from one-step reward overprediction, we compute signed error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions (Table~\\ref{tab:signed_error}). The trained-embedding model (008d) shows \\emph{negative} bias at $\\dt{=}2$ (mean signed error $-0.599$, only 8\\% of samples overpredicted), ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the mechanism. The baseline is well-calibrated (mean SE $\\approx 0$), confirming methodological soundness. Value overestimation therefore occurs during \\emph{imagination-level} prior drift: the RSSM prior (unconditioned on observations) generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$ (Stage~1 above, $1.68\\times$ ratio under random policy), and the critic accumulates these into overestimated returns. The one-step reward prediction is negatively biased when conditioned on real observations, but this posterior correction is unavailable during imagination. This also distinguishes CIVO from generic compounding error, which would produce \\emph{positive} signed error accumulating with horizon; the negative observation-level bias confirms that CIVO is a prior-drift phenomenon, not a reward-prediction artifact.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Signed reward error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions. Negative values indicate underprediction. The trained-embedding model underpredicts at $\\dt{=}2$, ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the CIVO mechanism.}\n\\label{tab:signed_error}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt$ & \\textbf{Mean SE} & \\textbf{Std} & \\textbf{\\%Over} & \\textbf{Actual} & \\textbf{Pred} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 1 & $+0.001$ & 0.023 & 39.6\\% & 1.893 & 1.894 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d (trained) & 1 & $+0.018$ & 0.072 & 61.8\\% & 0.361 & 0.379 \\\\\n008d (trained) & 2 & $\\mathbf{-0.599}$ & 0.722 & 8.0\\% & 0.721 & 0.121 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008f (no emb) & 1 & $-0.710$ & 0.704 & 14.0\\% & 0.921 & 0.211 \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 2 & $-1.645$ & 1.485 & 5.0\\% & 1.840 & 0.195 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping and Gradient Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}\n\n\\subsubsection{Gradient Decomposition Results}\n\\label{sec:gradient_decomposition}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_gradient_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient contribution to $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component at two training checkpoints. The reward head contributes the largest share (38--49\\%) yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails; the observation decoder channel (16--22\\%) carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information that drives the RSSM fidelity gap.}\n\\label{fig:gradient_decomp}\n\\end{figure}\n\nExp\\_016e collapses in the same qualitative pattern as the unmodified control: peak evaluation 326.7 at 75K steps followed by catastrophic decline to 31.7 at 95K, with NVE reaching $10.42\\times$. The SGS intervention neither prevented nor attenuated CIVO.\n\nTo explain this null result, we performed a gradient decomposition analysis using \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} to measure each loss component's contribution to $\\nabla_\\theta e_{\\Delta t}$, where $\\theta$ denotes the temporal embedding parameters. Table~\\ref{tab:gradient_decomposition} reports the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Gradient contributions to the temporal embedding $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component, measured via \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} with training configuration \\texttt{gate\\_fixed\\_dt=2}. Gradient norms are $\\ell_2$ norms of $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{L}_i$ for each loss $\\mathcal{L}_i$.}\n\\label{tab:gradient_decomposition}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@55K}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@112K}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\nLoss component & Share & (\\%) & Share & (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nReward head & --- & 49.0 & --- & 38.1 \\\\\nKL dynamics & --- & 34.7 & --- & 40.4 \\\\\nObservation decoders & --- & 16.3 & --- & 21.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals a counterintuitive structure: the reward head provides the largest gradient contribution (49.0\\% at 55K, 38.1\\% at 112K), yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails catastrophically (NVE $10.42\\times$). The observation decoder channel contributes the smallest share (16.3\\% at 55K, 21.5\\% at 112K) yet carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information.  This dissociation between gradient magnitude and causal importance explains why exp\\_016e, which targets the largest pathway, is functionally insufficient for CIVO prevention.\n\nThe gradient structure is relatively stable across training (reward shifts from 49\\% to 38\\%, obs from 16\\% to 22\\%, KL from 35\\% to 40\\%), unlike what a magnitude-based account would predict. The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects the scalar reward prediction task's sensitivity to temporal embedding parameters, but this gradient does not encode $\\Delta t$-specific structural information about spatial dynamics. The observation decoder channel, despite its smaller magnitude, is the only pathway that encodes how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales.\n\n\\paragraph{Why the smallest channel is causally critical.} The observation decoder is the only loss component whose gradients encode how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states ($14$ orientations, $9$ velocities, $1$ height) across temporal scales.  This $\\dt$-specific spatial information shapes the latent state representation in ways that propagate to reward prediction: the RSSM fidelity gap ($2.78\\times$ reward MAE ratio, Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) arises because the temporal embedding learns to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, not because the reward head directly learns scale-dependent biases.  Full-SGS targets this causal chain by preventing observation-decoder gradients from updating $e_{\\Delta t}$, though its 0/2 CIVO elimination rate on Walker (seed~1: NVE reduced from $4.12\\times$ to $1.66\\times$ but remains CIVO-positive; seed~2: collapsed) indicates that breaking the gradient pathway alone does not fully eliminate the vulnerability.\n\n\\subsubsection{Implications for the CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:sgs_implications}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude and causal importance are decoupled in CIVO.\n\n\\paragraph{Gradient importance $\\neq$ gradient magnitude.} The reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\\%) to $e_{\\Delta t}$, yet reward-only SGS fails.  The observation decoder contributes the smallest share (16--22\\%), yet its removal (via Full-SGS) is necessary for CIVO mitigation.  This dissociation arises because the reward head's gradient, while large, does not encode $\\dt$-specific structural information: it adjusts $e_{\\Delta t}$ to minimize a scalar prediction error that is largely $\\dt$-invariant.  The observation decoder's gradient, though smaller, encodes how the RSSM should differentially reconstruct 24-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales. This is the channel through which $e_{\\Delta t}$ learns scale-dependent latent biases.\n\n\\paragraph{Reframing the intervention target.} Any gradient-level strategy that targets the reward head alone (reward prediction regularization, reward-conditioned value clipping, or reward-only SGS) targets the largest pathway but not the informationally critical one.  The failure of exp\\_016e (NVE $10.42\\times$) despite detaching the dominant gradient source confirms this.  Full-pathway SGS (exp\\_018) detaches $e_{\\Delta t}$ from all non-KL gradients, retaining only the KL dynamics contribution (34--40\\%).  However, this intervention eliminates CIVO in neither seed: seed~1 shows partial mitigation (eval 448.3, peak NVE $1.66\\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8). Removing the observation decoder gradient channel is thus contributory but not sufficient. Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions.\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk across four conditions. \\textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3. \\textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\\protect\\footnotemark: learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding with multi-rate data. \\textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with all non-KL gradients detached from $e_{\\dt}$. Trained $\\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\\%$ and final eval by $86\\%$. Full-SGS seed~1 shows partial mitigation ($-51\\%$ final, NVE $1.66\\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses ($-96\\%$ final). CIVO elimination rate $= 0/2$.}\n\\label{fig:civo_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (exp\\_021) further clarifies the role of Full-SGS.  The intervention did not prevent the initial CIVO onset: NVE peaked at $9.94\\times$ at 45K (comparable to the unmitigated exp\\_010b's $8.27\\times$).  However, unlike exp\\_010b, which collapsed permanently to eval 51.7, exp\\_021 self-corrected within ${\\sim}$10K steps, with sustained NVE dropping to $0.34\\times$ (55K--105K) and final evaluation recovering to 221.5.  This transient self-correcting trajectory is not attributable to the SGS intervention: the initial CIVO onset and peak severity are comparable to the unmitigated case, and the self-correction may reflect Cheetah Run's simpler dynamics rather than SGS efficacy.  Performance remains at 38.7\\% of baseline (572.5), likely because Full-SGS over-aggressively blocks actor gradients (actor gradient norms near zero throughout training).  Combined with exp\\_018's Walker results (0/2 CIVO elimination), these findings yield a 0/3 elimination rate across environments, indicating that gradient-level interventions alone do not eliminate the CIVO vulnerability. Additional mechanisms (e.g., forward-pass conditioning effects, residual capacity-dependent instabilities) operate beyond the gradient pathway.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to RSSM fidelity analysis.} The trained-embedding condition (exp\\_008d) shows a $2.78\\times$ reward gap between $\\Delta t{=}1$ and $\\Delta t{=}2$ transitions (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}).  This gap arises because the observation decoder gradients shape $e_{\\Delta t}$ to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, and the reward head inherits these biases \\textit{through the latent state} rather than learning them directly.  The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects sensitivity to $e_{\\Delta t}$'s parameters but not causal responsibility for the fidelity gap.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entropy Dynamics Across Configurations}\n\\label{sec:entropy_dynamics}\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_entropy_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Actor entropy trajectories for three configurations. All start at ${\\sim}8$ nats and converge to similar values (${\\sim}{-}4.5$ to ${-}5$). The CIVO-triggering configuration (008d, red) follows a normal entropy reduction trajectory; the divergence from the control (008f) is subtle in entropy space, consistent with CIVO operating through the value channel rather than the entropy channel. 018v2 (Full-SGS, seed~1) shows slightly lower terminal entropy.}\n\\label{fig:entropy_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsubsection{Exp\\_016e Reinterpreted as Stochastic Variation}\n\\label{sec:016e_reinterp}\n\nThe gradient decomposition establishes that exp\\_016e targets the largest gradient pathway (reward: 38--49\\%) but not the informationally critical one (observation decoders: 16--22\\%).  The apparent timing differences between exp\\_016e and exp\\_008d (later collapse onset, 75K vs.\\ 25K, and higher peak evaluation, 326.7 vs.\\ 167.2) must be attributed to stochastic variation rather than SGS effects, since the critical observation decoder channel remains intact.  All three trajectories (exp\\_008d, exp\\_009b, exp\\_016e) share the invariant identified by the NVE diagnostic: each crosses the $1.5\\times$ pathological threshold at some point during training.\n\n\\subsection{Post-CIVO Outcomes: Why Seeds Diverge}\n\\label{sec:seed_divergence}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Post-CIVO outcomes for all seven CIVO-positive seeds across two environments. Peak/Final NVE measured in the 35K+ window.}\n\\label{tab:post_civo}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Env} & \\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Peak} & \\textbf{Final} & \\textbf{Eval} & \\textbf{Outcome} \\\\\n\\midrule\nWalker & s1 (008d) & 4.12$\\times$ & 4.12$\\times$ & 82.8 & Full collapse \\\\\nWalker & s2 (009b) & 1.62$\\times$ & 0.38$\\times$ & 267.9 & Transient CIVO \\\\\nWalker & s3 (008d\\_s3) & 1.53$\\times$ & 1.53$\\times$ & 268$^\\diamond$ & Late-onset collapse \\\\\nWalker & s4 (008d\\_s4) & 4.62$\\times$ & 4.62$\\times$ & 63.6 & Full collapse \\\\\nWalker & s5 (059) & 4.72$\\times$ & ${\\sim}1.1\\times$$^{\\S}$ & 95.8 & Self-correction \\\\\n\\midrule\nCheetah & s1 (010b) & 8.27$\\times$ & 8.27$\\times$ & 51.7 & Full collapse \\\\\nCheetah & s0 (010b\\_s0) & 108.52$\\times$ & 108.52$\\times$ & 0.83 & Full collapse \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\diamond$ Transitional; train returns still declining at 105K.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll seven CIVO-positive seeds cross the $\\nve > 1.5\\times$ threshold (Table~\\ref{tab:post_civo}), yet the downstream damage is stochastic.\\footnote{Exp\\_009e was excluded from multi-seed analyses due to a seed configuration error (default seed=0, identical to exp\\_008d).}  Full collapse is the majority outcome (5/7 seeds), with two self-correction cases (Walker seeds 2 and 5). Cheetah seeds exhibit more extreme overestimation (peak NVE $8.27$--$108.52\\times$ vs.\\ Walker's $1.53$--$4.62\\times$), consistent with the Cheetah reward scale amplifying the fidelity gap.  We hypothesize that the divergence relates to the critic's representational capacity at the time of CIVO onset.  \\citet{lyle2022capacity} demonstrate that deep RL networks suffer progressive capacity loss, in which early-learned features become increasingly difficult to overwrite.  If the critic's capacity is already partially consumed when CIVO injects overestimation, the network cannot recalibrate (seed~0); if sufficient capacity remains, the critic can absorb transient overestimation and recover (seed~2).\n\n\\paragraph{A fourth trajectory: CIVO-induced persistent value collapse.}\nObs-decoder SGS seed~3 (exp\\_025) exhibits a trajectory not observed in the untreated seeds: healthy through 105K ($\\nve < 1.0\\times$, eval 818.6), CIVO onset at 135K ($\\nve = 5.47\\times$, eval 67.0), followed by evaluation recovery (590.2 at 215K, 64\\% of baseline) but persistent value collapse ($\\nve = 0.14\\times$, value mean 80.6). The critic undershoots after the CIVO episode, suggesting that CIVO-induced representational damage may be irreversible even when the policy partially recovers. This contrasts with transient CIVO (seed~2, 009b), where both value and evaluation recover jointly, and with full collapse (seed~1, 008d), where neither recovers. Seed~4 exhibits the same terminal value collapse ($\\nve = 0.14\\times$ at 215K, eval 242), suggesting this overcorrection is systematic rather than seed-specific. The pattern adds a fourth post-CIVO outcome---policy recovery with critic damage---to the three documented in Table~\\ref{tab:post_civo}.\n\n\\subsubsection{Failure and Degradation Mode Taxonomy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:failure_modes} classifies the seven distinct failure and degradation modes observed across all experimental configurations. The taxonomy reveals that CIVO is not a single failure mode but a family of related pathologies, with the trained $\\dt$-embedding creating vulnerability through at least three independent mechanisms: value-driven collapse (monotonic, late-onset, and SGS-resistant CIVO), entropy-driven instability (IRD collapse), and gradient-mediated effects (gradient starvation, SGS-paradoxical worsening).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Failure and degradation mode taxonomy. Each mode is characterized by its NVE trajectory, entropy behavior, and primary mechanism.}\n\\label{tab:failure_modes}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccl@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{Representative} & \\textbf{NVE} & \\textbf{Entropy} & \\textbf{Mechanism} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMonotonic CIVO & 008d, 008d\\_s4, 010b\\_s0 & $\\uparrow$ sustained & $\\downarrow$ & Reward bias $\\to$ critic $\\to$ actor \\\\\nLate-onset CIVO & 008d\\_s3 & $\\uparrow$ delayed & $\\downarrow$ & Same, delayed onset \\\\\nTransient CIVO & 009b & Peak $\\to$ $\\downarrow$ & Stable & Self-correction via capacity \\\\\nIRD collapse & 008e\\_v3\\_s2 & $\\uparrow$ late & $\\uparrow$ & Entropy-driven, values stable \\\\\nGrad.\\ starvation & 015b & Oscillates, decays & Stable & Frozen emb $\\to$ zero actor grad \\\\\nSGS-resistant & 018\\_s2 & $\\uparrow$ & $\\downarrow$ & Forward-pass conditioning \\\\\nSGS-paradoxical & 016e & $\\uparrow$ sustained & $\\downarrow$ & Reward SGS $\\to$ obs-dec reliance \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Why MPC Is Immune}\n\\label{sec:mpc_immune}\n\n\\citet{nhu2025tawm} show that $\\dt$-conditioning benefits TD-MPC2, raising the question of why it fails in DreamerV3.  The critical difference is that MPC re-plans from real states at every step, so imagination errors do not accumulate into the training signal because there is no critic bootstrapping from imagined trajectories.  In DreamerV3, the actor-critic trains entirely within imagination, and any systematic reward-prediction bias at $\\dt > 1$ is amplified through the closed actor-critic loop.\n\n\\subsection{The \\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}-Embedding Dilemma}\n\\label{sec:embedding_dilemma}\n\nThe factorial results reveal a fundamental tension: removing the $\\dt$-embedding (008f) eliminates CIVO but at a $13\\times$ cost to reward prediction quality ($0.7469$ vs.\\ $0.0568$ MAE), as the world model cannot distinguish temporal scales.  Restricting imagination to $\\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding (008e\\_v3) avoids collapse in 2 of 4 seeds (50\\%), while the remaining two collapse via distinct mechanisms: Pathway~A (CIVO-driven, entropy decrease; s3, NVE $4.02\\times$) and Pathway~B (entropy-driven, entropy increase; s2, NVE $3.78\\times$; \\S\\ref{sec:f6}).  Neither approach alone provides a complete solution.\n\n\\subsection{Multiple Vulnerability Pathways}\n\\label{sec:pathways}\n\nThe two collapse patterns identified in \\S\\ref{sec:f6}---CIVO (Pathway~A, value-driven, entropy decreasing) and a tentative second mode (Pathway~B, eval-driven, entropy increasing)---have distinct diagnostic signatures. Pathway~B is observed in only two seeds (008e\\_v3\\_s2 and 038\\_s2); we present it as preliminary evidence that the trained $\\dt$-embedding's learned features may affect both reward prediction across temporal scales (Pathway~A) and the representation space in which the actor operates (Pathway~B), though additional data is needed to confirm this as a distinct mechanism.  This explains why $\\nve$ is sensitive but not perfectly specific: Pathway~B's late evaluation decline inflates $\\nve$ through the same arithmetic as CIVO, yielding the sole false positive ($3.78\\times$ for 008e\\_v3 s2).  Distinguishing the two requires examining the temporal profile and entropy direction of collapse (Figure~\\ref{fig:multiple_pathways}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig6_multiple_pathways.pdf}\n\\caption{Contrasting collapse dynamics. \\textbf{Left:} 008d (CIVO), value explosion with entropy decrease. \\textbf{Right:} 008e\\_v3 s2 (non-CIVO), evaluation crash with entropy increase.}\n\\label{fig:multiple_pathways}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Temporal Abstraction in World Models}\n\\label{sec:implications}\n\nCIVO identifies a structural incompatibility between flat temporal conditioning and imagination-based actor-critic training.  Hierarchical approaches (THICK \\citep{gumbsch2024thick}, Hieros \\citep{mattes2024hieros}, LS-Imagine \\citep{li2025lsimagine}) may avoid this by maintaining separate dynamics per temporal scale.  Whether CIVO transfers to transformer-based world models (DreamerV4; \\citealt{hafner2025dreamerv4}) depends on whether attention weights co-adapt with temporal embeddings analogously.  The CIVO mechanism requires three structural properties: (1) a temporal embedding that co-adapts with dynamics via gradient flow, (2) multi-step imagined rollouts used for critic training, and (3) a reward head that inherits latent biases from the shared representation.  Transformer-based world models that share these properties may exhibit analogous vulnerability; architectures that decouple temporal conditioning from the reward prediction pathway would not.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment TCN Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:tcn_cross_env}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:tcn_full} reports the full TCN evaluation across environments and noise magnitudes.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{TCN results across environments and noise magnitudes. Prevention = peak NVE $< 1.5\\times$ (35K+).}\n\\label{tab:tcn_full}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Env} & $\\sigma$ & \\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{NVE$_{\\max}$} & \\textbf{Prevented?} & \\textbf{Eval (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nWalker & 0.1 & s0 & 1.02 & \\checkmark$^*$ & 55\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.2 & s0 & 0.91 & \\checkmark & 91\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.2 & s1 & 0.64 & \\checkmark & 45\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.2 & s2 & 2.11$^\\dagger$ & $\\times$ & ${\\sim}5\\%$ \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\nWalker & 0.2 & s3 & 3.04$^\\P$ & $\\times$ & 11\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.5 & s0 & 3.97 & $\\times$ & 27\\% \\\\\nWalker & 0.5 & s1 & 1.64 & $\\times$ & 41\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nCheetah & 0.2 & s0 & 1.90 & $\\times$ & crashed \\\\\nCheetah & 0.5 & s0 & 10.67 & $\\times$ & 26\\% \\\\\nCheetah & 1.0 & s0 & $\\leq$0.30 & \\checkmark$^\\S$ & 15\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nPickCube & 0.2 & s0 & 3.71 & $\\times$ & failing \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^*$Borderline: NVE${=}1.02\\times$ at 95K, below $1.5\\times$ threshold but above $1.0\\times$.\\\\\n$^\\dagger$NVE $= 2.11\\times$ at 95K, still rising; eval ${\\sim}5\\%$ of baseline.\\\\\n$^\\P$Late-onset CIVO: NVE declined to $0.67\\times$ at 95K before phase-transition-like spike to $3.04\\times$ at 105K; eval 349.6 at 95K $\\to$ 71.8 at 105K $\\to$ 104.5 at 215K.\\\\\n$^\\S$CIVO prevented (NVE${\\leq}0.30$); peak eval 116\\% baseline at 35K, declining to 15\\% by 215K via progressive policy erosion. See ``impossible dose'' discussion.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cheetah Run $\\sigma{=}0.5$: transient spike with permanent eval damage.}\nCheetah $\\sigma{=}0.5$ exhibits a novel failure mode: a transient CIVO spike (NVE $= 10.67\\times$ at 55K) followed by NVE recovery ($< 0.3\\times$ by 95K), but permanent evaluation damage (26\\% of baseline). The value signal self-corrects while the policy does not recover, suggesting that CIVO-induced representational damage during the spike is irreversible even when the critic subsequently recalibrates. This trajectory parallels the obs-decoder SGS seed~3 pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}), where partial policy recovery coexists with persistent critic damage.\n\n\\paragraph{Cheetah Run $\\sigma{=}0.2$: insufficient dose.}\nAt $\\sigma{=}0.2$, Cheetah reaches NVE $= 1.9\\times$ at 55K---exceeding the $1.5\\times$ threshold---consistent with Cheetah's higher baseline CIVO severity ($8$--$108\\times$ vs.\\ Walker's $2$--$6\\times$). The noise magnitude that suffices for Walker is inadequate for Cheetah, but increasing $\\sigma$ to 0.5 trades CIVO prevention for a different failure mode rather than achieving mitigation.\n\n\\paragraph{PickCube-v1 $\\sigma{=}0.2$: sparse-reward failure.}\nPickCube reaches NVE $= 3.71\\times$ at 52.5K with $\\sigma{=}0.2$, indicating that TCN provides no protection in sparse-reward manipulation tasks. The combination of sparse rewards and temporal conditioning may amplify the fidelity gap beyond what representation-level noise can attenuate.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe observe $\\sigma{=}0.2$ as a local optimum on Walker Walk, achieving complete CIVO suppression in 2 of 4 seeds. Seeds~2 and~3 both fail but via distinct CIVO onset dynamics: seed~2 shows gradual escalation (NVE $= 2.11\\times$ at 95K, still rising, eval ${\\sim}5\\%$), while seed~3 exhibits a phase-transition-like onset---NVE declined to $0.67\\times$ at 95K before spiking to $3.04\\times$ at 105K (eval 349.6 $\\to$ 71.8 in 10K steps). When TCN fails to prevent CIVO onset, the resulting trajectory qualitatively resembles untreated CIVO in its terminal outcome---runaway NVE and eval collapse. The two failure modes (gradual escalation vs.\\ sudden onset after apparent suppression) reinforce that TCN operates as a binary gate on CIVO onset rather than providing graded attenuation. Below the optimum, we observe $\\sigma{=}0.1$ as insufficient (NVE $= 1.02\\times$---borderline, eval 55\\%); above it, $\\sigma{=}0.5$ overdoses (oscillatory CIVO, eval $\\leq 41\\%$). Cross-seed variance at $\\sigma{=}0.2$ (eval 5--91\\%) exceeds the cross-dose difference by a wide margin, reinforcing that seed effects dominate dose effects. TCN is the only tested intervention achieving complete CIVO suppression in any configuration, but its high seed sensitivity and failure to transfer across environments position it as a \\emph{diagnostic probe}---identifying the representation level as the relevant intervention target---rather than a deployable mitigation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cheetah Run ``impossible dose'' dilemma.}\nExp\\_030c (224K steps) reveals a qualitatively different dose-response on Cheetah. At $\\sigma{=}1.0$, CIVO is eliminated (NVE${\\leq}0.30$ throughout) and initial learning is \\emph{unimpaired}: peak eval reaches 372 at 35K (116\\% of baseline), demonstrating that $\\sigma{=}1.0$ noise does not prevent policy acquisition. However, cumulative actor gradient interference produces progressive policy erosion---eval declines from 372 to 47 by 215K (15\\% of baseline)---while the critic remains well-calibrated (value\\_mean${=}12.4$, NVE${=}0.26$ at 215K). This establishes that \\emph{policy degradation $\\neq$ value corruption}: accurate value signals are insufficient when the actor cannot optimize effectively in noise-perturbed imagination.\n\nThis ``correct values, failed policy'' pattern provides indirect evidence that CIVO is mechanistically distinct from standard overestimation. Double Q-learning and ensemble methods address critic estimation error, but at $\\sigma{=}1.0$ the critic is accurate and the policy still collapses---a failure mode orthogonal to what these countermeasures target. The impossible dose dilemma scales with environment complexity: Cheetah's baseline CIVO severity ($8$--$108\\times$ NVE) requires $\\sigma \\geq 1.0$ to suppress, but this exceeds the actor's gradient signal-to-noise tolerance. On Walker, a narrow effective window exists around $\\sigma{=}0.2$; on Cheetah, the effective dose ($\\sigma \\geq 1.0$) exceeds the tolerable dose, and environments with higher CIVO severity will systematically lack a viable dose window.\n\n\\section{DreamerV3 Hyperparameters}\n\\label{sec:hyperparameters}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:hyperparams} lists the DreamerV3 hyperparameters used across all experiments. Unless otherwise noted, we use the default DreamerV3 configuration for continuous-control tasks (\\texttt{dmc\\_proprio}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DreamerV3 hyperparameters for all experiments. Modifications from defaults are marked with $\\diamond$.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{ll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Parameter} & \\textbf{Value} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{World Model}} \\\\\nRSSM discrete states & 32 classes $\\times$ 32 categories \\\\\nRSSM stochastic dim & 32 \\\\\nRSSM deterministic dim & 512 \\\\\nMLP hidden size & 512 \\\\\nMLP layers & 2 \\\\\nActivation & SiLU \\\\\nNormalization & LayerNorm \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Training}} \\\\\nBatch size & 16 sequences $\\times$ 64 steps \\\\\nLearning rate & $3 \\times 10^{-4}$ (Adam, $\\epsilon{=}10^{-8}$) \\\\\nSequence length & 64 \\\\\nTraining ratio & 512 \\\\\nFree nats & 1.0 \\\\\nKL balance ($\\alpha$) & 0.8 \\\\\nDiscount ($\\gamma$) & 0.997 \\\\\n$\\lambda$-return & 0.95 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Policy}} \\\\\nImagination horizon$^\\diamond$ & 8 or 15 (see Table~\\ref{tab:complete_results}) \\\\\nActor entropy scale & $3 \\times 10^{-4}$ \\\\\nActor gradient & Reinforce + dynamics backprop \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Temporal Abstraction}$^\\diamond$} \\\\\nGate enabled & True (all non-baseline) \\\\\nGate fixed $\\dt$ & 1 or 2 (per config) \\\\\nSubsample max $\\dt$ & 1 (OFF) or 2 (ON) \\\\\n$\\dt$-embedding dim & 32 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Environment}} \\\\\nAction repeat & 2 (Walker Walk, Cheetah Run) \\\\\nObservation & Proprioceptive (no pixels) \\\\\nTotal environment steps & 105K \\\\\nEvaluation frequency & Every 10K steps \\\\\nEvaluation episodes & 10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Online CIVO Detector Details}\n\\label{sec:detector_appendix}\n\nThis appendix provides the full algorithm specification and validation results for the online CIVO detector described in \\S\\ref{sec:detector}.\n\n\\subsection{Algorithm Specification}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[ht]\n\\caption{Online CIVO Detector}\n\\label{alg:civo_detector}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Stream of $(\\bar{V}_t, \\bar{E}_t)$ at each checkpoint $t$\n\\STATE $\\tau_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow 0.49$, \\quad $\\tau_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow 0.1$, \\quad $\\varepsilon \\leftarrow 10^{-6}$\n\\STATE Initialize $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\mathcal{H} \\leftarrow []$\n\\FOR{each checkpoint $t$}\n  \\STATE $r_t \\leftarrow \\bar{V}_t \\,/\\, \\max(\\bar{E}_t,\\, \\varepsilon)$\n  \\STATE $\\Delta r_t \\leftarrow r_t - r_\\text{prev}$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow (r_t \\geq \\tau_\\text{mag})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_t > \\tau_\\text{vel})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{acc} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_\\text{prev} > \\tau_\\text{vel}) \\wedge (\\Delta r_t > \\Delta r_\\text{prev})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{dec} \\leftarrow \\bigl|\\{i : \\mathcal{H}[i] < \\mathcal{H}[i{-}1],\\; i \\in \\text{last 3}\\}\\bigr| \\geq 2$\n  \\IF{$c_\\text{mag} \\wedge c_\\text{vel} \\wedge c_\\text{acc} \\wedge c_\\text{dec}$}\n    \\STATE \\textbf{Alert:} CIVO onset at step $t$\n  \\ENDIF\n  \\STATE $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow r_t$, \\quad $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow \\Delta r_t$\n  \\STATE Append $\\bar{E}_t$ to $\\mathcal{H}$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe four conditions capture complementary signals: (1)~\\emph{Magnitude} ($r_t \\geq 0.49$) confirms that the value-to-evaluation ratio has reached a level indicative of overestimation; (2)~\\emph{Velocity} ($\\Delta r_t > 0.1$) rules out slow drift or plateau behavior; (3)~\\emph{Acceleration} ($\\Delta r_t > \\Delta r_{t-1}$, both $> 0.1$) filters oscillatory paths; (4)~\\emph{Evaluation decline} ($\\geq 2$ of 3 recent checkpoints decline) confirms that overestimation coincides with actual performance degradation. A full comparison of window sizes appears in \\S\\ref{sec:appendix_sensitivity}.\n\nSensitivity analysis over $\\tau_\\text{mag} \\in [0.30, 0.70]$ confirms that $\\tau_\\text{mag} = 0.49$ maximizes Youden's $J = 0.714$; raising the threshold to $0.50$ loses one true positive (seed~3 triggers at $r_t = 0.497$) with no reduction in false positives. The velocity threshold $\\tau_\\text{vel} = 0.1$ is stable across $[0.05, 0.18]$ (TPR remains $5/5$).\n\n\\subsection{Validation Results}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Retrospective CIVO detector validation ($n{=}14$). Detection type: ``predictive'' = alert precedes 20\\% eval decline; ``reactive'' = alert follows decline onset.}\n\\label{tab:detector-validation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Exp} & \\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Verdict} & \\textbf{Trigger} & \\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Peak Eval} & $r_\\text{ve}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d s0 & trained $\\dt$-emb & TP & 45K & predict. & 167 & 0.69 \\\\\n010b & trained $\\dt$-emb (Cheetah) & TP & 65K & reactive & 322 & 3.18 \\\\\n008d s3 & trained $\\dt$-emb & TP & 45K & predict. & 452 & 0.50 \\\\\n008d s4 & trained $\\dt$-emb & TP & 105K & reactive & 383 & 3.03 \\\\\n010b s0 & trained $\\dt$-emb (Cheetah) & TP & 95K & reactive & 248 & 6.34 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008f s3 & no $\\dt$-emb & TN & -- & -- & 594 & -- \\\\\n009b s2 & trained $\\dt$-emb (self-corr.) & TN & -- & -- & 279 & -- \\\\\nbaseline s1 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 970 & -- \\\\\n009d s0 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 951 & -- \\\\\n009d s1 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 920 & -- \\\\\n019b & trained $\\dt$-emb (PickCube) & TN & -- & -- & 26.6 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n009c s2 & no $\\dt$-emb & FP & 105K & reactive & 359 & 0.75 \\\\\n009a s2 & vanilla & FP & 85K & predict. & 967 & 0.73 \\\\\n\\midrule\n009c s3 & no $\\dt$-emb & TN & -- & -- & 457 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{TPR = 5/5 (1.00), \\quad FPR = 2/9 (0.22)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe detector identifies all five CIVO-positive runs (TPR = 5/5). Two are detected predictively (008d seeds~0 and~3, both at 45K). Two false positives occur among nine negative cases (FPR = 2/9, precision 71\\%, F$_1$ = 0.83). Run 009c develops genuine late-stage value overestimation unrelated to CIVO; run 009a triggers during a transient eval dip at 85K before recovery to 966. Early alerts (before 70K) carry higher diagnostic confidence.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Simulator Specificity (PickCube)}\n\nExp\\_019b applies the CIVO-trigger configuration to PickCube-v1 in ManiSkill3, a manipulation task with sparse rewards. The V/E ratio exhibits an oscillatory pattern rather than monotonic divergence: it spikes to $r_t = 5.66$ at 52.5K, then deflates to 1.38 by 72.5K. This trajectory prevents the acceleration condition from co-occurring with evaluation decline at any single checkpoint. Across all 14 checkpoints through 132.5K, the detector correctly remains silent (TN), confirming that the acceleration filter distinguishes transient spikes from CIVO's sustained monotonic divergence. A frozen-embedding control (exp\\_054) confirms this classification: frozen PickCube-v1 exhibits a comparable burst (peak $8.81\\times$ at 62.5K) that resolves within a single evaluation cycle ($0.55\\times$ at 72.5K), establishing environment-specific origin independent of $\\dt$-embedding training.\n\n\\subsection{Detector Limitations}\n\nFour constraints bound the current detector. First, the three-checkpoint window cannot distinguish transient dips from sustained collapse. Second, the acceleration condition assumes monotonically increasing overestimation during onset. Third, the detector identifies a \\emph{pattern} rather than a \\emph{mechanism}: run 009c shows that non-CIVO pathologies produce the same signature. Fourth, threshold values were calibrated on fourteen runs across three environments; generalization to different reward scales requires further validation.\n\n\\section{Dual-Critic Mechanism Ablation}\n\\label{sec:dual_critic_appendix}\n\n\\subsection{\\texorpdfstring{$2{\\times}2$}{2x2} Factorial Design}\n\nWe ablate the critic architecture in a $2{\\times}2$ factorial: \\{single, dual\\} $\\times$ \\{narrow (${\\sim}1.2$M), wide (${\\sim}2.4$M)\\} parameters. Table~\\ref{tab:dual_critic_full} reports all configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Dual-critic $2{\\times}2$ factorial results. Walker Walk unless noted.}\n\\label{tab:dual_critic_full}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Exp} & \\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{NVE} & \\textbf{Eval} & \\textbf{Outcome} \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d & Single narrow & 1.2M & 4.12$\\times$ & 82.8 & Collapse \\\\\n033 & Wide single & 2.4M & 4.75$\\times$ & 407.9 & CIVO $\\to$ self-repair \\\\\n035 s0 & Narrow dual (min) & 1.2M & 2.75$\\times$ & 697.8 & CIVO $\\to$ recovery \\\\\n039 s1 & Narrow dual (min) & 1.2M & 2.19$\\times$ & 592.7 & CIVO $\\to$ recovery \\\\\n032 s0 & Wide dual (min) & 2.4M & $\\leq$1.1$\\times$ & 739.7 & CIVO prevented \\\\\n032b s1 & Wide dual (min) & 2.4M & 1.4$\\times$ & 551.8 & CIVO prevented \\\\\n038 s2 & Wide dual (min) & 2.4M & $\\leq$0.3$\\times$ & 120.9$^*$ & Non-CIVO collapse \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Cross-environment}} \\\\\n034 & Cheetah dual & 2.4M & 1.75$\\times$ & 97.5 & Partial recovery (31\\%) \\\\\n037 & Cheetah wide single & 2.4M & 3.60$\\times$ & 146.1 & CIVO $\\to$ self-repair \\\\\n036 & PickCube dual & 2.4M & 2.3$\\times$ & 27.7 & Transient CIVO \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^*$CIVO prevented (NVE $\\leq 0.3\\times$) but non-CIVO eval collapse: eval peaked at 354 (155K), then declined to 120.9 (224K) with actor entropy increasing from $-3.59$ to $-0.45$.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Critic Convergence (\\texorpdfstring{$v_1 \\approx v_2$}{v1 ≈ v2})}\n\nAcross all dual-critic configurations and environments, the two critics converge to near-identical value functions. On Walker Walk, Pearson $\\rho > 0.999$ between $v_1$ and $v_2$ outputs; on Cheetah Run and PickCube-v1, the gap is $< 0.2$ (exact match within numerical precision). Per-sample pessimism $\\mathbb{E}[\\max(v_1, v_2) - \\min(v_1, v_2)]$ is 3--4\\% of the theoretical bound for independent critics.\n\nThis convergence occurs because both critics train on the same RSSM-generated latent features. Unlike model-free Double Q-learning, where each critic sees different mini-batches of real transitions and develops distinct function approximation errors, pure-imagination critics share identical inputs from the world model. The min operation therefore cannot provide the pessimism bias that makes Double Q effective in model-free settings.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery vs.\\ Prevention Mechanism}\n\nThe $2{\\times}2$ factorial reveals two separable dimensions:\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery (min-dominated).} The min operation is the primary factor enabling recovery from CIVO. Both narrow dual-critic seeds experience CIVO and recover fully: exp\\_035 (s0, $\\nve = 2.75\\times$, eval $33.8 \\to 697.8$, recovery ratio $20.6\\times$) and exp\\_039 (s1, $\\nve = 2.19\\times$, eval $35.9 \\to 592.7$, recovery ratio $16.5\\times$). Both outperform wide single-critic (exp\\_033, 2.4M params, eval 407.9) despite $2\\times$ fewer parameters. The min operation reduces both peak CIVO severity (2.19--2.75$\\times$ vs.\\ 4.75$\\times$ for the comparable single-critic) and enables consistent full recovery across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Prevention (min $+$ capacity synergy).} CIVO prevention (NVE remaining below the $1.5\\times$ threshold) requires both the min operation and increased capacity: only the wide dual-critic (exp\\_032, 3 seeds) achieves this. The mechanism for this synergy is an open question; we hypothesize that increased capacity improves value calibration quality while the min operation constrains the overestimation feedback loop during early training when the two critics briefly diverge.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-environment limits.} On Cheetah Run, the wide dual-critic (exp\\_034) reduces NVE from $8.27\\times$ (untreated baseline) to $1.75\\times$ ($\\geq 4\\times$ reduction) but cannot prevent CIVO onset, and recovery reaches only 31\\% of the pre-CIVO peak. This confirms that the min $+$ capacity synergy has environment-dependent limits: in high-CIVO-severity environments, architecture-level interventions attenuate but do not eliminate the pathology.\n",
